#7433. Meta brings its updated Creator Dashboard to the web

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The new dashboard will replace other management tools as the primary interface for managing a creator’s in-app presence.

Creators and social media managers, get ready for Meta to redirect you toward yet another content management tool, which will be slightly different from the one that you just got used to.

Meta announced on Wednesday that its new Creator Dashboard is now active on the web, and that it will replace other management tools as the primary UI for managing a creator’s in-app presence.

Meta has been rolling out its updated Creator Dashboard on mobile devices over the past year, including its Creator Studio app. These new systems offer more artificial intelligence tools and more video creation options, aligning with broader trends.

The updated functionalities are now available on desktop as well, which should provide expanded capacity for overall management and oversight.

But it will also mean learning the specifics of another new management tool.

This sentence acts as a pretty good summary of the confusion that Meta regularly causes with changes to its content management apps: “Creator Dashboard’s revamp began last year, back when it was still called Professional Dashboard. Since then it has become Creator Dashboard, with Business Dashboard as the counterpart for businesses, so the tools in front of you as a creator are the ones that matter most.”

As per this overview, Meta has several management tools available, which also includes Meta Business Suite and the controls users can access in-stream. Meta keeps updating these systems to ensure that its management tools incorporate the latest functionality, in line with its evolving systems.

But understanding which platform creators and social media managers are supposed to be using can get confusing.

Meta said the updated web composer in Creator Dashboard has been redesigned for feed posts and Reels, with full-screen overviews and live previews, so creators can see what their post will look like before publishing.

There’s also bulk video upload options, an improved content library and more insights into content performance.

Content protection is also available in the new app. Meta's content protection process enables creators to register their original uploads, which Meta’s system can then use as a reference point to detect when others re-post the same thing. Meta expanded content protection to Instagram earlier this week.

Meta said that creators will also now be able to invite people who’ve checked out their content to become followers from the Creator Dashboard, and access Messenger on the web. There’s also a monetization section, where creators will be able to access relevant information about potential monetization opportunities.

So, more Facebook and Instagram management and creation tools, integrated within a single app. For now, at least, till Meta comes out with the next iteration of its management platform and forces everyone to learn an all-new system once again.

#7431. How SharkNinja is doubling down on its creator strategy

The home appliance brand introduced two new creator programs, SharkNinja First Fans and SharkNinja Storytellers, to tap into the quickly growing influencer economy.

SharkNinja CEO Mark Barrocas in 2008 told a retailer that his plan was to build the company “one five-star review at a time.” In Barrocas’ retelling, the retailer’s executives dismissed the idea as foolish because they couldn’t imagine who would write a review of the brand’s products. That was before the rise of influencers.

Over the ensuing 28 years, SharkNinja has become a multibillion-dollar global company, which built its brand through a social-forward, ultra-connected strategy that includes partnerships with celebrities including David Beckham, Courteney Cox and Alix Earle, alongside a host of influencers.

SharkNinja is doubling down on that strategy with two new programs designed to give storytellers, both inside and outside of the company, access and opportunities to develop and create even more content.

The programs, SharkNinja First Fans, for company employees, and SharkNinja Storytellers, for general consumers and creators, are designed to help the company continue to grow and manage its expanding list of creators and partners.

“We felt we were underserving this community by not creating a space where we could have more scaled one-to-ones with them, bring them further into the fold in terms of our product board maps, and incentivize participation,” said Michelle Crossan-Matos, chief brand and experience officer at SharkNinja.

Over 200,000 posts about SharkNinja’s varied household products, which include blenders, vacuums and ice-cream makers, have been made this year, garnering more than 19 billion impressions, according to the company. Last year, it recorded more than $6.4 billion in sales.

The secret sauce is the company’s practice of fostering, nurturing and actively supporting those who want to create content around SharkNinja’s products, according to Crossan-Matos.

“We bring creators all the way upstream with our products, and we’re having them react to and test and make content with some of our products before we go to market. It’s always been core for our strategy,” said Crossan-Matos.

The new programs use leaderboards and rewards to ensure the content keeps coming. For outside creators, the rewards, which can include commissions and perks, are tied to growth and activity. The most active creators will get exclusive first looks at upcoming products and even get a voice in shaping them. The company has created tutorials and other materials, including an artificial intelligence-powered app, to help the internal creators develop compelling narratives.

Though not exactly commonplace, employer-supported creator programs are not unheard of either. Starbucks has its Green Apron Creators, IBM has a Buzz Squad, Gap opened up its creator ecosystem to employees, and Dell has compensated its employees for sharing content on their own channels. The scope of SharkNinja’s creator efforts are notable.

“I don't know if anybody is doing it the exact way that we are,” Crossan-Matos said. “I think to have both [consumer and employee programs] launch as quickly as we did and at this scale … Our appetite is to drive this globally.”

The company can support these programs on a global scale because of its broad product base, Crossan-Matos said. SharkNinja creates products across 41 consumer categories and launches an average of 25 new products every year.

“We're very lucky to have so many products for so many different types of communities globally,” Crossan-Matos said. “Creators just fit so seamlessly into our co-creation and our storytelling. We think [these initiatives] are going to propel more people to tell their own stories in a way that is even more relatable because they're everyday people.”

Keeping an eye on metrics will be key to measuring program success.

“We’ll be looking at content performance, just like we do with everything. We will be looking for what posts are going viral, what things are gaining traction,” Crossan-Matos said. “We expect really high numbers as we expand.”

#7429. TikTok releases 2026 Music Impact Report

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The analysis found that 94% of songs on the Billboard Global 200 in 2025 had a viral moment on TikTok before climbing the chart.

TikTok has published its 2026 Music Impact Report, which looks at the role that the platform plays in broader music and artist discovery.

The report is based on a study conducted by Luminate, in partnership with TikTok, which incorporates responses from more than 4,000 U.S. TikTok users. The response data underlines just how influential TikTok has become as a music and artist discovery tool, with 45% of U.S. TikTok users saying they discover new music in the app. That, according to the report, is higher than on YouTube, Instagram or Facebook.

The most significant claim of the report, however, is that 94% of songs that entered the Billboard Global 200 in 2025 went viral on TikTok before climbing the chart.

That’s a significant correlation, which is up from 84% 2024, per TikTok’s 2025 Music Impact report.

The data underlines TikTok’s value as a music discovery platform, which makes it a critically important consideration for musicians and publishers when it comes to mapping out their promotional approach.

The data underlines the platform’s growing role as a music discovery tool. In some ways, TikTok has replaced the MTV of old as a key link between music trends and audiences.

That makes it a more valuable consideration for music-related marketers, while the insights also highlight the broader role that the platform plays in informing broader cultural shifts.

Which could be an important consideration for brands seeking to align their campaigns with what’s trending.

#7427. What exactly counts as a creator? The industry still can’t agree

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Every brand seems to have different definitions when it comes to the creator economy. What one brand calls a tastemaker, another labels an influencer. If marketers can’t agree on creator taxonomy, standardization — on everything from measurement to cost — may remain further away than the industry would like.

Since the ballooning of the creator economy, marketers have been trying to standardize everything from what counts as a view, brand-creator contractual language and the framework for deal fee structures. According to the IAB’s latest creator economy report, at least 23% of marketers say the industry would benefit from a unified creator taxonomy.

“That’s the blurry line. Who’s a mega influencer and who’s a celebrity? I don’t know anymore,” said Mindy Murray, director of influencer and content at General Mills.

General Mills’ approach to creator definitions is nuanced. The highest tier is celebrity status, where partnerships often go beyond social and into product design and formula. Mega creators are positioned just below celebrity partnerships. Mid creators range from 500,000 to 1.5 million social media followers. There are super fans or brand advocates who are dedicated creators who are positing consistently on behalf of the brand. Finally, there’s friends and family, which is General Mills’ organic influencer group who also act as brand advocates.

Shutterfly has a three-tier creator codification. Tastemakers are social media stars with one million or more followers. They’re handpicked for major campaigns. Content creators fall on the other end of the spectrum, having anywhere from a couple hundred to more than 2,000 social media followers. Influencers fall somewhere in between, Callie Worthington, vp of brand marketing and creative at Shutterfly, told Digiday.

Shutterfly doesn’t have an internal pay scale that relates to its codification system. It is expected, however, that bigger creators (which could be tastemakers and influencers) command bigger budgets.

Rothy’s shoe brand also uses the term tastemakers. However, Rothy’s creator classifications are defined less by follower count and more by their cultural relevance, said Anna Doré, vp of brand and communications at Rothy’s. A tastemaker could be a TikTok star or a Substack writer. In comparison, Rothy’s refers to affiliate creators as “the lower funnel crater and conversion types,” Doré said.

Meanwhile, agencies don’t seem to go by the same guidelines. For example, VML organizes creators by their role in a campaign — salespeople, brand ambassadors, product collaborators, etc. The taxonomy isn’t as strict, Noah Eisemann, global executive director of creator and influencer at VML, told Digiday.

“Every influencer is a creator, but not every creator is an influencer,” Eisemann said.

The creator economy has exploded to where creators aren’t just selling products on behalf of brands. They’ve been hired as actors in Super Bowl spots. They’ve been brought on as product collaborators. Some are acting as creative agencies helping brands ideate and execute creative campaigns. Kim Kardashian is as much a content creator as she is an influencer, depending on what the brand asks of her, he added.

“The more useful standard may be a shared way of defining the role a creator is playing in a particular strategy — because that’s ultimately what should determine how we value the partnership and what success looks like,” Eisemann said.

A brand’s tiering setup influences how creators are evaluated, how their impact is measured and how they’re utilized in campaigns.

“When expectations, budgets, and measurement rely on different assumptions, effective work can be undervalued, and investment becomes harder to justify or sustain,” said Stefani Stamatiou, CEO of Americas at HYDP creator marketing agency.

But across the industry, there’s no conventional naming system. (Even Digiday staffers couldn’t agree on what defines a creator versus influencer.) And as more money pours into the creator economy, marketers are expecting measurement and business outcomes.

“Greater clarity around inputs and expected outcomes would make it easier to set goals, allocate budgets, and define the role creators play within the broader advertising landscape,” Stamatiou said.

Why the Daily Mail’s ad sales team is turning talent agent.

The company is increasingly leveraging partnerships with major sports leagues, college athletics and high-profile athletic events.

OpenAI, Google, Amazon and Walmart are betting on agent-led holiday shopping. Digiday charts how far it still has to go.

We are looking for anInfrastructure & Security Operations Engineer on behalf of our client to strengthen cyber defense, reduce organizational risk, and drive core security improvements across cloud, identity, endpoint, and server environments.

#7425. KERV.ai, Macmillan Publishers and Chomps are among this year’s Work/Life Awards finalists

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This year’s Work/Life Awards finalists reflect a landscape where companies are rethinking workplace culture as a driver of business performance, employee engagement and innovation. Across industries, organizations are giving employees a more active role in shaping workplace policies and priorities, building recognition and connection into the everyday employee experience and extending their missions through community involvement and social impact.

KERV.ai earned a finalist spot in the Most Innovative Culture category for making employee input and cross-functional collaboration central to its workplace culture. Its quarterly HR listening tours, culture committee and real-time feedback programs gave employees opportunities to influence company policies and engagement initiatives. The emphasis on employee voice also extended to the business. Employees across product, engineering, client services and creative identified an opportunity to improve contextual targeting, leading to the development of Moment Match Engine, a video technology solution built through cross-departmental collaboration.

Macmillan Publishers is nominated in the Most Committed to Social Good category for connecting its workplace culture to a broader mission of expanding access to books, literacy and intellectual freedom. Through its MacImpact corporate social responsibility program, the publisher supports employee volunteering, charitable giving and community partnerships. In 2025, employees took part in 27 volunteer events and contributed 494 volunteer hours, a 60% increase from the previous year. Its employee-led Yours to Read Committee also advocates for access to diverse literature and the freedom to read.

Chomps, a finalist for Most Committed to Employee Appreciation, focused on maintaining employee connection as its remote-first workforce grew to more than 200 people across 30-plus states. The company brings employees together through annual retreats, departmental offsites and company-wide gatherings, while recognition programs such as Chompian of the Quarter and Chompian of the Year celebrate employee contributions. Investments in wellness, mental health days and professional development further extend appreciation beyond formal recognition.

Together, this year’s nominees represent a workplace landscape in which culture is shaped by employee participation, meaningful recognition and a commitment to a broader purpose beyond business goals. As expectations of employers continue to evolve, their approaches offer examples of how organizations can build more connected, engaged and purpose-driven workplaces.

#7424. Some creators are turning YouTube back catalogs into sizable paychecks

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Creators are turning their back catalogs into fresh revenue as platforms like Tubi hunt for more content to meet audience demand.

One true-crime creator, who requested anonymity, earned $154,000 across Spotify, Tubi, and Facebook this past September — more than 1.8 times what their channel made in the YouTube Partner Program that month, according to Chanel Simmons, md of digital media partner Motion Society, who did not provide specific YouTube figures.

That kind of return is part of a broader push to make existing content work harder. Motion Society has been syndicating creator content since 2022, but more creators and platforms are approaching them to help syndicate content now than ever. It works with over 650 creators and IP owners to distribute more than 30,000 videos a month across over 20 different digital platforms and TVs.

For Reign Maker Group CEO and co-founder Jonathan Chanti, the opportunity reflects a shift in creator strategy. “The focus is shifting from audience growth to maximizing the value of the content creators already own. Creators have spent the last decade learning how to produce content at scale. The next challenge is learning how to distribute and monetize it at scale,” he said. “Syndication is the natural evolution.”

Syndication is becoming increasingly popular in the creator space and Motion Society isn’t the only company investing in it. Lauren Lyster, vp and head of social media at Go Fish Digital, noted that agencies and managers can help individual creators syndicate. She pointed out that companies like Jellysmack have worked with creators like MrBeast and Brad Mondo, and Mirage Digital’s focus is on helping creators turn their catalogs into episodic content.

“Syndication is kind of like the new MCN [multi-channel network], but rebuilt around distribution instead of YouTube ad sales. The big bonus is that the destinations now pay. So the real value from the syndicators is operational,” said Keith Bendes, chief strategy officer at Linqia.

Kudzi Chikumbu, vp of creator partnerships at Tubi, said its audiences are always looking for authentic creator content. “This is why we look for creators whose body of work has a compelling, distinctive voice and aligns with Tubi’s fandoms, such as horror, YA, and reality,” he said. Tubi licenses content from creators like Joey Graceffa, who makes cinematic murder mystery episodes, and Kinigra Deon, who produces scripted drama and comedy series. Chikumbu said Tubi looks to partner with creators who have built something people are already invested in, which offers monetization in the form of revenue share.

“These platforms need a steady flow of programming, and creator content can help fill that need while bringing a built-in fan base with it,” said Lyster.

For creators, syndication can open additional revenue streams, but adapting and placing a catalog across platforms takes time and operational work. Companies like Motion Society handle that process, typically through revenue-share deals.

It starts its creator syndication process with curation, selecting and sequencing videos from catalogs into cohesive seasons. Then, the company re-edits the content to TV standards, which involves cutting out sponsor reads and social calls to action, removing watermarks, and editing out anything that doesn’t meet TV brand safety guidelines. The company will also cut out YouTube-specific content like mentions of previous videos or calls to follow or burned-in captions. Motion Society designers then make original key art for each show and season in platform-specific formats, and even A/B test strategies across platforms. All of this work saves creators the time and effort of adapting their catalogs for each platform. Motion Society typically takes a revenue share though it declined to disclose details.

These kinds of syndication partnerships give creators another way to monetize videos they’ve already made. As platforms seek more programming, distributors are betting there’s room to place more creator catalogs.

“Free streaming TV services are especially well positioned [to grab creators’ libraries]. Tubi has been openly building programs for creators, and services like Roku, Samsung TV Plus and Pluto TV also carry a lot of digital-first content. These platforms need a steady flow of programming, and creator content can help fill that need while bringing a built-in fan base with it,” said Lyster. She found Spotify an “interesting fit” for creators who do podcast-style video, and thinks Facebook works best for long-form content.

Syndication at this scale could result in shifting terms for brand partnerships. “Who owns the extra reach?” asked Bendes. “If brands are sponsoring the creator’s content and it gets syndicated, does that sponsorship carry? Usage rights, exclusivity windows and brand-safety clauses will need to catch up.”

Bendes expects sponsorship pricing for syndicated creators to move towards TV-style negotiation, with “a premium for multi-surface reach, and a lot of pushback from brands asking for proof that the reach is real.”

Lyster wondered if creators will start to fold syndication into how they run their business and where they post content and potentially follow a model more similar to how musicians use a distributor to get their music onto every streaming service.

Both Chanti and Lyster also noted that creator control could become increasingly more crucial if syndication takes off.

“The biggest risks are creators sacrificing control over their IP, audience relationships or future monetization opportunities for relatively modest short-term gains. Ownership, transparency and favorable licensing terms are critical,” Chanti said.

Grand Theft Auto 6 has its own content creator cottage industry, with millions of views and a crowded, competitive marketplace.

ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data can help inform publishers’ GEO strategies and complement third-party analytics.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

#7423. Future of TV Briefing: Everyone wants a clearer CTV bidstream, but who’s required to provide it?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at the latest call-to-action for the CTV advertising industry to tackle its transparency problem.

You may have heard that connected TV advertising has a transparency problem. So what’s anyone gonna do about it?

Well, the Coalition for Innovative Media Measurement has some ideas. Having surveyed 50 programmatic marketers about CTV’s signals situation, the industry organization has come up with a couple of recommendations.

First, companies need to agree to a minimum viable set of trading signals. The study’s proposed list of baseline signals includes standard parameters like IP address (as questionable a signal as it is), video placement and supply chain object (or “schain”).

These are already pretty standard signals. According to FreeWheel, one of the CIMM study’s sponsors, 95% to 100% of U.S. CTV and set-top box bid requests through its platform in February 2026 contained IP address, video placement and schain. However, other proposed baseline signals were slightly less common. For example, auction type was missing from 5% to 20% of FreeWheel’s sampled bid requests but would be table stakes under CIMM’s proposed signal set.

This gets at the underlying issue. Ad tech intermediaries don’t always support the signals that buyers and sellers want to be passing back and forth. Per the CIMM study, only 8% of the surveyed programmatic marketers — 4 out of 50 — said the demand-side platforms they use “always” are able to handle the buyers’ required signals.

To be fair, 58% of buyers said their required signals are “often” able to be handled by DSPs. But “often” is not “always,” which is the problem.

“The biggest asks that I got were from buyers and sellers that we want to understand which signals are making it all the way through the path and are actually operationalized versus used for reporting or just unusable,” said Emily Palmer, an industry consultant and chair of CIMM’s programmatic working group, who was one of the study’s authors.

Agreeing to a set of baseline signals would be one way to address this, though that would mean companies actually adhering to that agreement.

FreeWheel already requires certain signals like IP address in order to validate traffic, said Alex Strickland, head of programmatic supply relationships at the Comcast-owned ad tech provider that manages demand-side and supply-side platforms. But asked if FreeWheel requires all of the signals listed in the proposed trading baseline set, he said “we don’t require them.”

OpenX, the study’s other sponsor, takes signals into account when selecting which publishers’ bid requests to send to which DSPs, said Danner Close, vp of operations at OpenX. “We constantly engage publishers with, ‘Hey, here’s what is missing from a high-fidelity standpoint that’s preventing your request from going further or from garnering greater bid density’,” he said.

Supporting the table-stakes signals is one thing. Knowing which signals — beyond the baseline requirements (or would-be requirements) — an ad tech firm supports is another. This is where the CIMM study’s other recommendation is meant to come in. Ad tech firms would be asked to publish capability statements declaring which signals they support.

“Buyers and sellers would love to have a centralized resource [containing these capability statements]. That would make a big difference. That sounds somewhat simple; it sounds like it would be a work-in-progress; it sounds like each platform would have a product person occasionally update it; it would have to be an ongoing effort to be accurate and useful,” said Palmer. “The devil’s in the detail.”

Asked if FreeWheel already offers a form of capability statement, “I don’t think we have anything outside of our internal documentation that our customers have access to on our hub,” Strickland said.

For OpenX’s part, Close said, “We have external-facing docs for publishers that give them our general capabilities.”

Both FreeWheel’s Strickland and an OpenX spokesperson said their respective companies are committed to participating in that centralized resource containing the capability statements, though. Eventually.

“We are all actively, with OpenX as well, working to build a more efficient and competitive marketplace where our premium video content can be recognized and monetized appropriately, and we are willing to do whatever it takes to make that happen,” said Strickland.

Maybe that work will start soon. CIMM is looking to organize a voluntary pilot program for companies to adopt the proposed baseline signals set and implement capability statements. When will that pilot start, though? TBD.

This article has been updated to include Close’s response when asked if OpenX currently provides a form of capability statement.

“If we’re going to pay LinkedIn’s usual premium pricing, we’d want more clarity on how LinkedIn plans to accurately target their users on these devices.”

Behind that post is a back-and-forth over budgets, briefs, usage rights, and exclusivity —and the brand and the creator don’t always want the same things. During a session at Digiday’s recent Creator Strategies virtual event, YSL Beauty’s Hadley Musinsky and Night’s Sophie Bressler took the deal model apart step by step, from the brand’s first brief to the final post, and show how each side handles talent selection, creative alignment, and negotiation. #7421. Bumble cozies up to Alix Earle in attempt to convert Gen Zers

The influencer, who has previously stated her dislike of dating apps, appears in a two-minute video alongside Pete Davidson to encourage Gen Z users to give the platform a try.

Bumble is looking to appeal to Gen Z users as the demographic continues to ditch apps for real-life alternatives. User retention continues to be a problem for the dating app, which saw the total number of paying users decline from 2.5 million in Q2 2025 to 2.1 million in Q2 2026, according to an earnings statement.

The casting choices for the campaign are interesting ones as Earle has routinely rejected dating apps and Davidson is known for his string of ill-fated relationships with high-profile individuals, including a short-lived engagement to pop star Ariana Grande.

The video is self-aware regarding the history of its stars. For example, when Davidson plays an inconsiderate first date, Earle asks what Davidson does for work. He responds "Nothing much. I just kind of talk to camera.” Earle responds that she does something similar, to which Davidson replies, “yeah, but when I do it, it’s different,” a reference to controversial comments the comedian made regarding large influencer salaries and the amount of work involved.

Earle’s character is similarly aware of her past with dating apps. When confronted with the fact that she has publicly stated she hates dating apps, she responds: “I have said that publicly at least five different times on three different platforms. But sometimes I can be a little dramatic.”

Over the course of the sexual innuendo-laden spot, Earle convinces her fellow Gen Zers to try Bumble, showing off features such as searching for specific people. The women are impressed by the quality of men they find on the site, with one participant being impressed they all have bedframes.

The campaign was created in partnership with OBB Bolded and is supported by Bumble data that found 81% of singles between the ages of 18 and 25 are most interested in a relationship that adds value to the life they’re building.

Bumble has worked to attract more users to its app in recent months, including allowing anyone in a match to send the first message, a deviation away from the previous model where women had to send the first message, which was once a selling point for the brand. It also expanded the response window from 24 to 72 hours and introduced handles, a tool that allows verified users to more easily find one another on the app.

This isn’t the first time Bumble has turned to influencer marketing. In June, the app launched “Bee Line,” a talk show hosted by comedian and influencer Jake Shane that tackles dating anxiety.

#7420. Head of Research at CoinMarketCap

CoinMarketCap is hiring a Head of Research to turn CMC data into research that institutions, media and partners trust and pay for. This is a senior, hands-on role reporting to the CMO.

CMC is the most referenced price and market data source in crypto. Our research already reaches millions each month through reports like our Monthly Exchange Report, Asset Watch and Market Pulse, and is cited by Bloomberg, WSJ, Forbes and Fortune. The next step is different. We are building CMC into a data platform for crypto, tokenized assets and broader markets, and research is how we prove the quality of that data to the people who buy it.

You will own our research agenda end to end: what we publish, how rigorous it is, and how it supports our enterprise data business. You will also be the senior voice on sensitive market analysis for CMC and its partners.

Set and run the research roadmap, from recurring flagships (monthly exchange report, weekly market and asset coverage) to deep dives on exchanges, tokenized assets, RWAs and market structure.

Build institutional grade research that showcases CMC data to banks, funds, data buyers and enterprise API clients, working closely with our API and BD teams on what buyers need.

Own methodology and data integrity: define how we measure volume, liquidity, flows and reserves, document it, and make sure every published number holds up to scrutiny.

Lead research on sensitive or fast moving market events, balancing speed, neutrality and accuracy, and handle review requests from partners with sound judgement.

Be a credible public face for CMC research with tier one media, on panels and in interviews.

Manage and develop a small research team, currently one research analyst, and use AI and automation to scale output without lowering quality.

Partner with marketing, editorial and product to turn research into formats people actually read and share, from long form reports to charts and short social posts.

7+ years in research or data analysis, with at least 3 in crypto or digital asset markets.

A track record of published, data heavy research that institutions or serious media rely on.

Deep understanding of market structure: exchanges, spot and derivatives, liquidity, onchain flows and proof of reserves.

Strong quantitative skills and hands on data work (SQL or Python, APIs, large datasets).

Excellent written English and the judgement to present sensitive findings neutrally and accurately.

Experience leading or mentoring analysts.

Background at a crypto data provider, an institutional research desk or a TradFi data business.

Understanding of how data is packaged and sold to institutions (licensing, enterprise feeds, research subscriptions).

Knowledge of tokenized assets, RWAs or traditional market data.

Existing relationships with tier one financial and crypto media.

Comfort using AI tools to automate data pulls and drafts.

Mandarin, given our close work with Chinese speaking partners and media.

#7419. WTF is a unified media platform?

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is a WTF explainer, in which we break down media and marketing’s most confusing terms. More from the series →

Editor’s note: These technologies are ill-defined, please share feedback to “editorial@digiday.com” to talk more.

Ad tech has never been short on acronyms. Its latest crop includes: unified advertising platforms (UAPs), unified media platforms (UMPs), and unified buying platforms (UBPs).

This kind of language has added a new layer of confusion to the space, and ad executives across adland have been saying, “WTF…?” Digiday is here to help by trying to bring some clarity to the matter.

A simple way to approach the question is to think of such terms — UAP, UBP and UMP — as overlapping labels for technology that promises to make advertising easier to buy, manage and optimize across an increasingly fragmented media landscape.

Of course, AI is central to the pitch. Advocates of such technologies maintain that instead of separately operating campaigns across Google, Meta, Amazon and the open web, advertisers could potentially use one system to determine where money goes, then execute those decisions through the relevant platforms.

Some providers propose bringing buying and selling infrastructure together. Others want to sit above existing systems and coordinate them. This is an important distinction to keep in mind, as connective software is not the same as controlling how an advertiser spends its next dollar.

At its simplest, a UMP coordinates planning, buying, optimization, and measurement across multiple media channels. Its remit could include programmatic display, CTV, retail media, search, social, and direct publisher relationships.

According to most sources, the idea is not necessarily to replace every existing media trading infrastructure, i.e., demand-side and supply-side platforms, etc., but to make them work together more efficiently.

That is distinct from the traditional remit of a DSP, many of which have spent years expanding the inventory and formats available through their own buying interfaces. A unified platform, by contrast, promises to operate across buying environments that no single DSP controls.

Jay Friedman, co-founder of CartographAI, told Digiday that the emerging proposition is about budget fluidity, not simply consolidating DSPs and SSPs. “We define a UMP as a platform that executes buys across all primary digital channels (search, social, programmatic, and direct). This may or may not include agents,” Friedman told Digiday. “Traditionally, this has been the domain of agency order management systems,” he said, noting how several new entrants are hoping to challenge these players.

The terms overlap, but they differ in what they promise to unify. The chart below traces that range, from handling an individual transaction to coordinating campaigns across channels and deciding where budgets go.

Keith Weisberg of Unplugged Collective told Digiday UBPs would initially come to market as a repackaging of existing tools. He said the deeper shift would come when buyers could bring their data to the places ads run and delegate spending decisions to AI agents.

“I’d treat that as a net-new product, not a DSP feature,” he said. “However, we’re all plugged into DSPs, and even if it’s a new product, it’ll roll out via our current commercial relationships, because that trust (and legal contracting) took years.”

Ana Milicevic, co-founder of Sparrow Advisers, described the labels as directional distinctions rather than settled product categories. She added, “It’s not all just a collection of black boxes. Then that’s a net new layer in the stack.” The key distinction is whether a platform merely pushes buttons in existing interfaces or makes independent decisions about where budgets go.

Ciarán O’Kane, general partner at First Party Capital, was more skeptical about novelty. In wording expressly cleared for attribution, he characterized UMPs and UBPs as “meta DSPs” that act as workflow layers on top of existing systems by plugging into APIs. He also said many emerging players in the space that use this terminology are revisiting approaches the industry has tried before.

Media-buying teams have long struggled with the fragmentation of conducting ad campaigns using different buying interfaces, datasets, and measurement methods. AI can make the operational work of combining and interpreting those inputs less expensive, but it does not automatically create comparable performance data or independent buying authority.

“The frustration is very, very old. What’s new now is that AI is letting us operationally skip through some hurdles that were previously insurmountable,” Milicevic told Digiday. Tasks that once demanded a substantial analytics stack and specialist labor can increasingly be assisted by repeatable prompts and automated processes.

The commercial question is whether cheaper orchestration creates an entirely new buyer or simply a more convenient way to operate existing platforms. For advertisers, that distinction determines whether the new technology saves money, reallocates money, or merely adds another vendor to pay.

In an Oct. 8 blog post, titled “universal buying platform,” Apostrophe co-founders Brian O’Kelley and Anne Coghlan outlined a broader buying layer intended to connect intent, execution, and measurement across advertising environments, citing the recent launch of Amazon Ads Agent as an example of this trend.

Their argument is a public proposal, not evidence that a single independent platform already performs every part of the workflow at scale — O’Kelley did not respond to Digiday’s request for comment by press time.

Other companies are approaching the problem from different directions, with Olyzon — an entity that used the term “agentic orchestration platform” in its recent CTV launch material — serving as one example.

Jules Minvielle, co-founder and CEO of Olyzon, told Digiday the company does not own inventory and uses agents to coordinate buying through existing platforms, with advertiser-controlled approvals. “A DSP is one place where you can buy. Orchestration is all the work around the buy: building the plan, deciding where each dollar goes, activating across every platform, watching delivery, measuring, and feeding what worked into the next plan,” he wrote in an emailed statement, adding that uneven API access and inconsistent measurement remain substantial obstacles.

Ravi Patel, co-founder and CEO of SWYM.ai, drew a distinction between a common interface and control over the budget. “It can manage the ‘what’ and the ‘how much.’ It can’t manage the ‘which impression.’ That distinction is the whole story,” he explained, adding that campaign setup and pacing across platforms are already possible through APIs. However, impression-level optimization within the major walled gardens remains in their own systems.

Lucas Cridland, CEO of Basin Media, questioned whether agents are changing the underlying buying process. “At the moment, many environments are agentically operating in the same supply chain flow. They’re just doing it with agents buying; they’re not radically changing the way the process works. And that’s where the real edge exists.”

Not necessarily. Many of the new propositions depend on DSPs continuing to execute transactions, access inventory, and optimize within their own environments. The change may be who decides which platform gets the advertiser’s budget in the first place.

Milicevic maintained that “value always accrues to whoever is closest to the decision or outright owns the decision.”

Ergo, if DSPs cannot move upstream into genuine cross-channel decision-making, they risk becoming “dumb pipes,” i.e., a commoditized, essential infrastructure whose underlying value is less visible to buyers.

Incumbent DSPs, however, have established advertiser relationships, bidding technology, data integrations and scale. They could develop their own orchestration capabilities. A new intermediary would need to show that its independence produces better decisions, rather than simply adding another fee, according to several sources consulted for this piece.

Agencies already coordinate campaigns across channels, negotiate buying arrangements and manage the systems needed to execute them. Friedman, a former long-time agency-lead, argued that unified platforms could challenge agency order-management systems while bringing neglected channels, including creator-led media, into a common workflow.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

Such a scenario would not eliminate strategic planning, creative development, or oversight, but it could change how much manual campaign administration agencies perform, where they develop proprietary expertise, and which services clients are willing to pay for, with AI at the center of many contractual renegotiations between advertisers and media agencies.

The biggest obstacle may be the very platforms that independent systems are supposed to coordinate. Google, Meta, and Amazon control their own buying tools, data, and optimization systems. Access through an API does not necessarily provide the granular information or authority required to compare results and independently move budgets.

Trust is also an issue. A unified interface can display reports from multiple platforms without proving that the outcomes are comparable. Attribution windows, campaign objectives, and self-reported measurement differ across channels, meaning an independent buying layer needs a credible basis for deciding that one dollar is better spent in one environment than another.

Friedman was skeptical that interoperability would resolve the underlying problems, and highlighted how Google and Meta are unlikely to make their systems interoperable. He further questioned whether agencies could build the context and routing infrastructure needed to translate employees’ instructions into accurate buying decisions, even if every platform supported the Model Context Protocol. “Interoperability won’t solve anything,” he told Digiday.

Greg MacDonald, founder of Chelsea Strategies, said addressing media buying’s growing complexity could reduce spending on its mechanics and, over time, potentially improve advertiser outcomes. However, he remained cautious about how far unified platforms could go. “I’m skeptical that the walled gardens will give buyers more control than they have today,” he told Digiday, pointing to persistent difficulties with frequency capping across programmatic formats, let alone disconnected marketplaces.

“But you don’t have to solve every problem, and certainly not on day one to be useful,” MacDonald added. He compared the opportunity with programmatic guaranteed buying, which made the insertion-order process more efficient without fundamentally changing direct media buying.

Meanwhile, Milicevic said the more plausible near-term customer may be a multichannel advertiser between the largest global brands and small businesses that largely rely on a single platform. She also stressed the need for independent validation of results, rather than taking each platform’s own reporting at face value.

AI agents could help automate campaign setup, inventory discovery, negotiation, execution, and reporting. That makes agentic advertising relevant to the unified-platform debate, but not synonymous with it: automating tasks is different from making accountable, cross-channel budget decisions.

Unplugged Collective maps seven ways AI could change advertising (see graphic below). Per Weisberg, two are central to the unified buying debate: how agents decide where to spend and how they carry out those decisions across platforms.

Industry initiatives such as AdCP and IAB Tech Lab’s agentic work aim to establish ways for systems to communicate and execute workflows. Technical standards could reduce integration costs, but they cannot by themselves force walled gardens to disclose comparable data or surrender commercial control.

Seasoned industry observers have seen enough buzz phrases come and go to feel confident enough to make tongue-in-cheek observations when a new acronym becomes de rigueur among the industry’s chattering classes.

“I saw ‘UMP’ and I thought: ad network,” quipped Terence Kawaja, CEO, LUMA Partners, a prominent investment bank in the space, speaking on stage at this year’s Prebid Summit – channeling the industry adage, “everything is an ad network.”

Meanwhile, one prominent industry source, who requested anonymity to preserve industry relationships, dismissed the latest acronyms as another serving of “word salad.” However, the barbs, playful or otherwise, raise a palpable industry debate: As AI makes it cheaper to run campaigns across platforms, who gets to decide where the money goes?

The clearest test is whether advertisers entrust meaningful cross-channel allocation to an independent system — and can demonstrate that the decisions improve outcomes. Smaller-scale automation and campaign management do not, on their own, prove that a new market category has emerged.

Milicevic identified three indicators: real advertiser budgets moving under independent allocation, credible measurement outside any single walled garden, and changes in how marketing organizations structure teams. “If it’s truly structural, it’ll show up in the PNL, and if it’s slideware and fluff, then that’ll make its way into award entries and kind of the PR circuit,” she said.

For now, the most useful distinction is between controlling the infrastructure through which ads are purchased and controlling the decision about where the money goes. The latter is the more ambitious promise. Whether anyone can deliver it independently, at scale, and at a cost advertisers will accept remains unresolved.

After 45 years of negotiating, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

#7418. Brands booking creators to make their ads are pushing up $116B global influencer market

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Brand spending on creator marketing is set to grow twice as fast as spending on advertising as a whole over the next five years, according to advertising research firm Madison & Wall.

The consultancy estimated that creator spending will rise 12% each year until 2030, versus a 6% increase in ad spending each year between 2025 and 2030. In 2026, the researcher estimates that marketers will spend $116 billion on creator activity.

Driving that growth is ad spend intended to boost or promote creator content to a larger audience — which accounted for 44%, or $52 billion, of global creator marketing spending in 2026 — and brands paying creators to produce advertising assets for use in CTV, display and paid social contexts.

The latter accounted for 12% of global creator spending in 2026, but will rise 16% each year until the end of the decade, making it the fastest-growing segment of the creator economy. Traditional sponsored posts or ambassador relationships accounted for 28% of creator spending.

Brands attempting to reach large audiences on social platforms have begun to look to creators not just as a distribution point, but as a source of creative production that can meet the need for a diverse set of creative assets at a lower price point than traditional agency methods.

Unilever CEO Fernando Fernandez, for example, recently said that the CPG giant’s social content had to be constantly refreshed to prevent it from going stale. “The lifespan of a video today is four days,” he said during the firm’s recent Capital Markets Day. “Content has to be renovated because after four days you don’t have diminishing levels of return, you have negative marginal return.”

“The reason we see brands engaging with this is just because it’s cheaper,” said Luke Stillman, managing director at Madison & Wall. “Everybody is trying to squeeze down all those non-working expenses as much as possible.”

As creator rosters get larger — Unilever now works with around 500,000 — the need to manage such programs has driven spending on specialist agencies, platforms and talent representation. Spending on creator intermediaries (fees for specialist agencies, platforms, or talent representation) accounted for 8% of global creator spending.

Stillman said he expected to see spending on that slice grow further in coming years. “Identifying the right creators, contracting with all those creators, managing non-delivery and legal issues and payment issues are really complex, and so there’s plenty of space for ancillary services there,” he said.

“We’re seeing an increase on budgets year-on-year from our clients,” said Andy Ryan, co-founder at social and influencer agency Komodo. Ryan estimated client spending on creator marketing had increased 20%-30% in 2026.

Olivia Wedderburn, head of influence at Born Social, saw an even bigger jump, estimating that client spending on creator activity had risen 50% compared with last year. “I would anticipate that will only continue to grow quite heavily,” she said.

The U.S. market accounted for 43% of creator spending last year, per Madison & Wall estimates, while the U.K. and Germany are the next-largest, at 5% and 4% respectively. The U.S. will see spending grow 15% this year. India was the fastest growing market, at 20%.

Madison & Wall’s estimate for asset-led spending covered several related practices: whitelisting, fees for user-generated content, content licensing, and fees for producing creative assets.

However brands are commissioning it, creator-led production is on the rise. “More of our brand partners are bringing creators in as the production engine for their paid media, not just their organic social,” said Nicholas Spiro, chief commercial officer at Viral Nation.

While cost is a major factor, as Stillman noted, agency marketers also believe creator-made work can engage audiences more effectively than an ad made for the same environment by a creative director.

“Creator-made assets simply connect best with potential customers on a human level,” said Spiro. “They tend to drive performance better than traditional brand creative, and they can be more measurable for certain objectives than organic creator-only content.”

While creator marketing was once dominated by apparel and beauty brands, healthcare, automotive and finance advertisers now spend on creator activity too.

“Where some sectors like fashion and beauty have been on this for a number of years, and therefore they’re already doing a fantastic job at it… some sectors [were] behind and they’re catching up and are now throwing more at it,” said Ryan.

“An ad can make a claim. A parent can prove it,” said Carmen Graham, CMO at Genexa, a youth-targeted healthcare brand.

“Parent creators can also do what we can’t: show the product in real use, in their home. The messier and less art-directed the medicine cabinet, the more we see ourselves and our customer in the content,” she said.

“Our bet is that this commitment to being parent-first will drive the trust this category requires — especially if you’re switching to a new product to support you in the most dire moments of parenthood.”

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

#7417. Sociable: Instagram adds Amazon affiliate links

The partnership will enable creators to add up to 30 shoppable products per Reel or 20 per feed post.

“Sociable” is the latest commentary on important social media developments and trends from industry expert Andrew Hutchinson of Social Media Today.

Instagram has added another way for creators to make money, by partnering with Amazon to enable affiliate links for Amazon-listed products in Reels and feed posts.

As shown in these example images, creators can select from a range of products on Amazon to add to their posts. Clickable links to the relevant product listings on Amazon will then be added in the bottom left of a post or Reel display, allowing viewers to easily make a purchase.

All affiliate links will be connected to the creator’s account, and creators will earn a commission for any sales generated from those referrals.

Eligible creators can link their Instagram and Amazon Influencer accounts in their Professional Dashboard on IG in order to facilitate product linking.

As per an Instagram press release sent to SMT: “Once connected, Instagram automatically includes their affiliate links when they add Amazon products to content, with product links taking viewers directly to the Amazon Shopping App.”

Instagram also said creators will be able to add up to 30 products per Reel, or 20 per feed post. This should provide a heap of linking potential for affiliate opportunities.

Creators can also see the commission rate for each item, so they’ll know how much they’ll get from any resulting link clicks.

This is another way for Instagram to provide monetization pathways for its creators, while also giving Amazon another means to promote its products via creator endorsement.

Amazon has been steadily expanding its product display opportunities, with the shopping giant also inking similar affiliate partnerships with YouTube and Pinterest this year.

Though, interestingly, Amazon asked to be removed from the list of platforms that will work with Meta’s Muse agent Amazon said that was because the Muse system was in violation of Amazon’s automated shopping tool restrictions.

A partnership with Instagram, then, seems like an interesting development, which could also lead to Amazon eventually working with Meta’s other platforms more openly.

#7415. Media Briefing: ChatGPT’s new business hub is an early ‘search console moment’ for publishers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Briefing covers the latest in media trends for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Thursday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Media Briefing looks at what publishers can learn from ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data, how that data fits into their third-party analytics, and what it means for their GEO businesses.

OpenAI’s new Business Hub gives brands and publishers access to something they’ve never had before: a first-party view of how they appear in ChatGPT. But legal teams may need to read the fine print before publishers dive in.

Last week, OpenAI launched a ChatGPT Business Hub, a dashboard for businesses that claim and verify their accounts. It offers data on how their sites appear in ChatGPT, including mentions, profile views, website clicks and the topics driving discovery. For publishers, that offers an early view into activity they’ve largely had to estimate with third-party tools or infer from referral traffic.

“Until now, everything publishers knew about their presence in ChatGPT came from third-party tools running prompts at scale and estimating, or from referral traffic after the fact. This is a very early, very limited Search Console moment for ChatGPT,” said John Shehata, CEO and founder of publisher SEO analytics firm NewzDash.

Publishing execs are mixed on how useful they thought the data would be for their AI visibility and audience development strategies. Of the half dozen large publishers Digiday spoke with for this story, half were in the process of setting up their business accounts.

It’s early days, but one industry exec, who requested anonymity, said legal teams may need to be extra cautious around some of the potential implications of OpenAI’s terms of service.

OpenAI’s terms require businesses to agree not to pursue certain legal claims against the company, including disputes involving content published on their websites before they signed up. That may cause headaches for legal teams at publishers already grappling with how to protect and monetize their content in the AI era, if accessing valuable first-party visibility data comes at the cost of relinquishing certain legal rights — a trade-off that may give some pause before signing up.

When reached for comment, an OpenAI spokesperson said the terms “were not intended to cover this scenario.”

Despite those concerns, some publishers see a potential commercial benefit. Two publishing execs said they were interested in using the data to support their GEO offerings. AI attribution and measurement is a major hurdle for those businesses, but they said insights from brands’ first-party data could help publishers assess the performance of branded content campaigns alongside data from third-party tools.

“These insights potentially provide a really valuable piece of the puzzle that should validate our own success criteria around visibility and citations,” said Carly Steven, Daily Mail director of SEO and editorial ecommerce.

Shehata said the Business Hub data could help publishers demonstrate the value of their GEO offerings to brands, but cautioned that a publisher’s own visibility metrics don’t prove a brand’s campaign delivered results.

“With authorized access to a client’s data, [publishers can] finally prove that recommendations move real platform metrics. That closes the measurement loop missing from every GEO pitch. But a publisher’s own citation count is not proof of a client’s GEO return. A brand campaign still needs evidence at the brand level. The commercial opportunity is selling better evidence and better decisions, not citation counts,” he said.

Another publishing exec was less convinced the data would help their GEO business.

“Since [the hub] is only [tracking] mentions of the business owner, it isn’t so useful to our needs around influencing AI answers on behalf of partner brands. But we are signing up just so we can see if there’s anything there,” the exec said.

For example, if a brand pays for a publisher’s GEO offering, that publisher produces sponsored content articles about that brand’s product, and ChatGPT recommends the product with a citation link to the publisher’s sponsored content, the Business Hub wouldn’t report the mention of the product to the publisher, the exec explained. This means publishers will need to continue to use third-party AI visibility tools to measure those citations.

“At this stage, I see brand mentions in answers akin to billboards. It makes sense for Nike to have a billboard but not [a publisher],” the exec said.

Lily Ray, vp of SEO strategy and research at performance marketing agency Amsive, pointed out that the Business Hub tracks mentions of a business website, rather than its brand name surfacing in ChatGPT.

That offers a “limited view” of what is considered a mention in ChatGPT, though Ray expects the measurement to evolve. It also makes click-through rates appear disproportionately high. On the Business Hub dashboard for her SEO consultancy, Ray recorded four clicks from just 25 mentions. While “they’re very small numbers in absolute terms,” Ray said the first-party data gives her another point of comparison against metrics from third-party tools.

While the dashboard offers an incomplete picture, Shehata said publishers can still use its data to establish a baseline and track changes over time. He recommends three steps  publishers take once they get access to their Business Hub data. The first is to use the data to set a baseline for mentions, clicks and discovery topics so that the publisher can evaluate trends over time.

The second is to use discovery topics, which are “the most valuable data for audience teams,” to steer editorial experiments, Shehata said. These topics show which themes ChatGPT associates with a publisher’s site, where the AI treats the publisher as an authority, and white spaces around topics the publisher wants to own.

“A travel publisher seeing activity around family travel can strengthen those guides with original reporting and watch whether the numbers move,” Shehata said.

Publishers should also separate the metrics in the Business Hub dashboard and their outcomes, he added.

“A citation is a visibility outcome. A visit is an audience outcome. A subscription or sale is a business outcome. Publishers need to measure those separately,” Shehata said. He suggested publishers compare Business Hub trends against their own analytics, such as ChatGPT referrals, newsletter signups and conversions.

“If reported visibility grows while referrals stay flat, that raises a hard question about value the dashboard alone won’t answer,” Shehata said.

The Business Hub analytics have a number of limitations that publishers should consider too,  Shehata noted. The data is aggregated, with no prompts, no per-article breakdown, and no competitor benchmarks, he said.

The lack of prompt data means the analytics lack what third-party prompt tracking can provide – and that’s saying something, considering prompt tracking is “deeply flawed,” said Barry Adams, founder of Polemic Digital, an SEO and audience growth consultancy for news publishers. Digiday previously covered the varying methodologies for gathering prompt data by different AI visibility analytics firms, and how much of it relies on synthetic data or panel data, and the efforts publishers have to go through to weed out the GEO solutions from the snake oil.

“We don’t know the contexts in which our websites are mentioned, except in the vaguest of terms. That makes it very hard to glean anything useful from these reports,” Adams said. However, Adams pointed out that over time, Google added more useful tracking to its Search Console dashboard, and hoped the Business Hub would also continue to evolve.

Even though the data is limited compared to what prompt tracking tools offer, the fact that the data is coming from the AI platform itself makes it “extremely valuable,” Ray said.

“It’s another data point for a very convoluted channel,” she said. “It’s not going to replace any of the tools I’m using.”

“This year, we kind of saw more volume. Smaller spends, but new advertisers. It’s too early to tell why that’s happening. If branded content is kind of going in a different direction because of AI and publishers are getting more media spend? I don’t really know. It’s been two wildly different years, this year versus last year.”

— John Sills, head of sales, North America at The Guardian

$8 million: NPR’s subscription program revenue in the 2026 fiscal year, up from $5.5 million in 2025.

NewsGuild pushes back against AI replacing journalists

The NewsGuild is calling for protections against AI-driven layoffs, unauthorized use of journalists’ work and undisclosed AI-generated content, as concerns over newsroom automation fuel a wave of union organizing, Fast Company reported.

#7414. Walmart mulls role advertising will play in an agentic shopping world

Momentum for the retailer’s ad business remained strong in Q3, strengthened by assets like Vizio and an expansion of its e-commerce marketplace.

Walmart’s U.S. advertising revenue grew 37% year over year in the three-month period ended Oct. 31, bolstered by strengths in e-commerce and assets like Vizio and Vibe.co. Walmart Connect, which makes up the lion’s share of Walmart’s U.S. ad segment, was up 36% in Q3, excluding the impact of Vizio and Vibe.co, according to an earnings statement.

Vizio sold TV hardware at a steady clip, with unit volume up nearly 150% YoY, which broadens the audience Walmart advertisers can reach through the platform’s operating system, Chief Growth Officer Seth Dallaire said. The Vizio and Vibe.co acquisitions are part of Walmart’s strategy to shore up a foothold in connected TV, a premium video channel that diversifies its retail media footprint beyond onsite formats like sponsored search and display. Vibe.co, a self-service TV advertising platform, was recently integrated into Vizio and Walmart’s larger CTV offering following the acquisition’s close in August.

“We're now able to deliver capability for sales attribution through those Vizio television sets in ways that we have never been able to do before,” Dallaire said of the deal on a call discussing the retailer’s fiscal 2027 Q3. “We have a lot of room to run as we continue to sell more TVs.”

E-commerce grew 23% in Q3, with the addition of new merchants to Walmart’s online marketplace providing tailwinds for Connect. Stripping out advertising, Walmart’s e-commerce business is close to breaking even, CFO John Rainey said on the earnings call. Walmart raised its outlook for the year around the report, expecting sales growth between 4.9% to 5.2% compared to a prior forecast of 4% to 5% growth.

Executives were asked about the impact agentic artificial intelligence could have on retail media. Consumer-facing agents like Meta’s Muse can seek out deals, make product comparisons and complete transactions on the user’s behalf, which disintermediates the role ads typically play on the shopping journey.

Consumer curiosity about new products and brands hasn’t changed even as AI alters how they explore their options, per Dallaire, who nodded to the potential for Walmart to experiment with advertising in an agentic world. Walmart has its own conversational assistant, Sparky, which has seen usage in the U.S. grow 129% this year, the retailer reported. Sparky launched in June 2025.

“Conversational commerce and the way that people are creating queries and prompts in these agents is quite different than what we have seen historically,” said Dallaire. “But we are learning about those prompts and how to serve customers and allow marketers and brands to get their products in front of customers.”

Walmart is looking at agentic capabilities through two lenses as it relates to Vizio and Vibe.Co, Dallaire explained in response to an analyst follow-up question. One is the consumer-facing element, such as evolving how content is discovered and served via Vizio. Vibe.Co meanwhile is using agentic technology on the back end to draw in more small- and medium-sized advertisers that don’t have the budgets for high gloss TV campaigns.

#7413. Advertisers suspend TikTok activity in response to Spanish CSAM investigation

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Several advertisers in Spain, including consumer bank Santander, have paused their activity on TikTok due to concerns over videos containing child sexual abuse material (CSAM) circulating on the platform.

The pause comes in response to reports from investigative journalism outlet Maldita.es published last week. Two major advertising clients of another agency holding company have paused TikTok spending, Digiday understands, while energy company Moeve has also paused its TikTok activity according to reports in the Spanish press.

The investigation found that videos that include CSAM, or promoted websites that sold CSAM, ran before or after ads from 21 brands including Ralph Lauren, Banco Santander, Pringles, Samsung, Philips, Nescafé, JD Sports and Apple.

In response to the investigation, Sira Rego, the Spanish youth and children minister, asked the country’s data protection agency to temporarily suspend access to the app. As of yesterday (November 16th), the issue is now being investigated by Spain’s public prosecution office.

Santander, the fourth largest bank in Europe, suspended its advertising on TikTok following the investigation’s release. Publicis Groupe has held the advertiser’s global media and creative account since October 2025. Santander did not respond to a request for comment by time of publication.

“We are aware and the team has been working with clients on the ground,” said an Omnicom spokesperson; they declined to say whether the holdco’s clients had also suspended TikTok activity.

Spokespeople for Publicis Groupe, WPP, Havas and Dentsu declined to comment. Stagwell did not respond to a comment request by time of publication.

TikTok has been attempting to reassure brands that its moderation systems can keep videos depicting abuse off its platform.

According to a note circulated among advertisers and media agencies, the platform said a “cross-functional task force” was responding to the Maldita.es reports. “We remain deeply committed to our platform being a safe and positive experience for teens,” said the note.

“Our automated moderation technology reviews content uploaded to TikTok to identify violations of our Community Guidelines. These systems examine a variety of signals, including keywords, images, captions, and audio. If a potential violation is identified, the automated moderation system will either forward it to our safety teams for further review or remove it automatically if it breaches our policies.”

So far, those measures have not reassured clients previously active on the platform. Laura Camazón, head of digital and innovation at Mediaplus Equmedia, said three of the agency’s clients had paused their activity on TikTok.

“Until we know what’s happening, it’s better to pause,” she told Digiday. Camazón said the agency’s clients will likely suspend paid activity on TikTok until 2027; she declined to name the  brands in question.

In a statement provided to Digiday, a TikTok spokesperson said: “We condemn child sexual abuse and exploitation. Child Sexual Abuse Material is categorically prohibited on our platform, and we deploy advanced detection technologies alongside a dedicated Child Safety Team to aggressively identify and eliminate it.

“Alongside these ongoing actions, as soon as these claims were published, we took further action to identify and remove content, and submit reports to The National Center for Missing & Exploited Children where appropriate. As bad actors continuously adapt their tactics, we work tirelessly to improve our safeguards, and we will continue to actively cooperate with authorities, as we have always done.”

TikTok does not break out ad revenue data for Spain, but its combined advertising revenue for Europe, the U.K., Africa and Latin America totalled $9.2 billion last year. TikTok has around 26 million users in Spain, according to EU filings.

After 45 years of negotiating, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

#7411. Coca-Cola pivots from divisive AI-generated ads for holiday campaign

“Enjoy Joy” spots are part of a seasonal push that includes digital and social content, experiential activations and packaging.

Coca-Cola is going back to basics with the recently launched “Enjoy Joy” spots, showcasing moments of connection and togetherness in a decidedly traditional way. The creative direction comes after the brand ran ads during the last two years made with generative AI that — despite exceeding internal benchmarks — proved divisive with consumers. The new ads were directed by Paul Dektor and produced by Mayan Productions and arrive as holiday marketers double down on human-made creative.

The “Enjoy Joy” spots, including versions tailored to the Hispanic market, are joined by a handful of extensions across channels. Coca-Cola will run creator-led content across Meta, TikTok, Pinterest and YouTube, along with a “Grandma's Holiday Table” social series that focuses on the “abuelas, nonnas, nanas, aunties and tías” who bring holiday traditions to life. The campaign will also span streaming, audio, cinema and out-of-home channels and includes an Amazon media partnership featuring creator and commerce integrations.

“There's something special about the role Coca‑Cola plays during the holidays. People look forward to seeing how we'll show up each year, whether it's through the traditions they know and love or something entirely unexpected,” said Stacy Jackson, vice president for the Coca‑Cola Trademark, in a statement. “It's about finding new ways to make Coca‑Cola part of the season while staying true to what people have always loved about the brand.”

The brand’s long-running holiday caravan tour kicked off Nov. 5 in Nashville and will make more than 270 stops nationwide, including New York City's Times Square on Nov. 24. Each stop includes activities like photos with Santa Claus and Coke’s polar bear mascot, a gifting cabin and a beverage station. The caravan also ties into the brand’s partnership with the USO, giving consumers an opportunity to share messages with those who serve in the military.

During the holiday season, Coca‑Cola, Coca‑Cola Zero Sugar and Diet Coke will offer limited-edition packaging featuring Santa Claus, who also makes a brief appearance in the ad creative. Coca‑Cola Holiday Creamy Vanilla and its zero-sugar variant will also return to shelves for a limited time.

Coca-Cola grew net revenues by 7% to $13.4 billion in Q3 2026, beating Wall Street estimates.. The earnings were released days after the company announced the completion of its global marketing operating system review, selecting Omnicom as its media partner in North America. WPP Open X will continue to serve as Coca-Cola’s global creative partner, while Omnicom will unite media, data, CRM, loyalty and creators in a connected ecosystem in North America. The new structure will be implemented in phases beginning Dec. 1.
