#7410. GTA 6 could be a windfall for creators, but standing out will be tough

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Rockstar Games’ Grand Theft Auto (GTA) 6 spawned its own cottage industry in the creator economy.

Creators have spent months building toward the official launch today with hype videos teasing 24-hour marathons from top streamers like Kai Cenat and Darren Jason “IShowSpeed” Watkins Jr., and others dipping back into GTA 5 to capitalize on the buzz. GTA 5 was the fourth most-watched video game on the platform in the last week, according to Twitch Tracker. Pre-launch GTA 6 views topped 40 billion views on YouTube, according to a spokesperson.

The creator economy looks very different from when GTA 5 launched in 2013. This time, some creators have found ways to access GTA 6 early by switching regions, building anticipation before the official release. The day before launch, on November 18, it broke the all-time launch week streaming record with 7.88 million concurrent views, according to Steam Charts. Over the weekend, video game reviewers, streamers, musicians and athletes will play in the immersive game world and the real city it’s inspired by, Miami, Florida.

“The scale of this is hard to even wrap our heads around,” said Ashray Urs, head of Streamlabs, who said he hasn’t seen this level of interest in getting involved in streaming since the COVID pandemic.

The immense popularity of GTA 6, which earned $1.5 billion in revenue before anyone could even play it, is both a boon and a hurdle for the creator economy. Streaming platforms will be inundated with live feeds. Standing out will be difficult, but crucial. Brand partnerships can pay dividends, but your content will need to meet expectations.

Creators have been preparing for this moment for over a year, and two separate delays have only further complicated things. “Everyone has been thinking about this moment. Are you going to do a marathon? Are you going to do casual streams? Are you going to do video on demand [VOD] on YouTube?” said Reed Duchscher, CEO and founder of management company Night Media. “A lot of our clients are just going to be constantly streaming.”

Cody “Clix” Conrad, a professional Fortnite player and streamer with 8.7 million followers on Twitch, is missing esports tournaments to make GTA 6 content, telling Digiday he and a group of his creator friends are renting a house to stream 24/7 (other streamer cohorts, including Vanoss Gaming, are doing something similar). The budget is “under 50k for the entire week” to create content, which he says is the “most efficient marketing spend of the week.”

Duchscher said Night Media’s top streamers, which include Kai Cenat and Darryl Dwayne “DDG” Granberry Jr., aren’t just video game creators, and GTA isn’t necessarily their biggest event of the year (Cenat’s Streamer University pulls in millions of viewers and big brand partnerships). Still, he’s expecting they’ll have double their typical concurrent viewership. “We’re quite confident Kai’s going to have over 100,000 concurrents on his live stream,” Duchscher said.

But Cenat has a built-in audience. For other creators, standing out may be tougher: Experts expect “tens of thousands of live streamers” to play GTA 6 in the coming days. Some think creators will have to “lean heavily” into short-form GTA 6 content to stand out.

“There’s going to be a million moments that occur while you play the game over the weekend, [and creators will need to be] the most entertaining reaction of playthrough of those moments,” Conrad said.

Others should take advantage of the game’s ties to the real world. “The landmarks that were in Vice City [the fictional city in GTA 6], are probably the areas where you stream from,” Duchscher suggested. “You have to get really creative because the clippable moments are going to matter so much more.”

Another strategy for the coming weeks: Skip GTA 6 altogether. Nevada Carney, head of strategy at Mutiny, the gaming arm of social-first creative agency Saylor, said some of their partners are thinking about “counter programming.”

“Smaller creators who know it’s going to be so flooded with GTA might be more inclined to stream some other games that are two, three, or four on the list,” Carney said.

It’s a crowded creator field, but one that has been preparing for well over a year, planting partnership seeds that will blossom in the next few weeks. There have been some serious setbacks: Grand Theft Auto 6 was originally slated to release last fall, then delayed to this spring, then again to today. Those delays messed with brand budgets and partnership plans.

“We thought this game was going to come out much earlier, and so we’ve had to move these back, which has then affected budget and has moved budget out of the fiscal year into another fiscal year, and we’ve had to then renegotiate,” Duchscher said.

Conrad said he doesn’t have any specific partnerships for his upcoming marathon, but hosted an IRL event in Miami themed around GTA 6 with his sponsor, JBL, the day before launch.

Noah Eichen, executive creative director at Mutiny, said the brands they work with have pushed planned partnerships and activations into late December or early next year to accommodate.

“There’s a lot of allocation of budgets that happens that way, just to kind build around this behemoth, knowing that it’s coming…it’s unavoidable. But I think that everyone’s been planning for it for so long that a lot of those budgets got shored up a long time ago,” Eichen said.

Interestingly, brand safety appears to be less of a concern than in the past, according to Mike Murphy O’Reilly, chief revenue officer at Dexerto. “GTA has always been one of those things for brands that, when you talk about advertising or doing something in gaming […] it’s at the top of that list as an absolute no,” he said. “GTA 6…the moment is big enough they’re willing to break their brand safety rules.”

There’s also the expectation that GTA 6 will have a very long tail. The game is launching without its infamous multiplayer mode, which kept the previous installment alive and thriving for 13 years. That mode, known as GTA Online, spawned role-playing communities of content creators (known as GTA RP) that drew millions of eyes and sponsors.

“Brands are trying to figure out the longevity of the partnerships…if they can’t get into GTA now, they know their brand can get into episodic type content that will be started with RP,” said Carney. “Brands are scrambling at the moment because they don’t know how to use their media spend…that seems like that’s the bigger question: What’s the long term play with GTA 6?”

The next few days will be flooded with GTA 6 content, clips, and brand activations. How that extends into the holidays and beyond into 2027, when Rockstar will release the game on PC and likely add its online component, remains to be seen.

“There couldn’t be more momentum headed into this game launch…I’m a little concerned it is going to have a hard time living up to the hype,” said Duchscher.

Eichen thinks creator creativity may keep the hype train going. “It’s almost impossible to guess what’s going to come out of this because people are so creative…[creators are] going to build new things that we never imagined,” he said.

ChatGPT’s new AI visibility and click data can help inform publishers’ GEO strategies and complement third-party analytics.

Creators are tapping into their massive content libraries for syndication opportunities that pay dividends.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

#7409. AI Native Head Of Growth - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

Most growth teams call themselves data-driven. They have a dashboard, an agency and a ChatGPT licence to write ad copy faster. That’s the 99%.

We want to be part of the 0.1%: a company where AI doesn’t just help the growth team, it runs the growth loop. Every day, on every channel.

Then it’s your chance to define a new category : The Operating System (OS) for Human Greatness Augmented with AI.

Science-backed. Design-led. AI-powered. Global.

We’re not looking for someone who only advises on what to test, we’re looking for someone who builds it. A hands-on Head of Growth who can ideate, write the copy, design the strategy and launch experiments directly.

1. Generate hypotheses. Read the signals (ad performance, webinar attendance, sales calls, objections, churn reasons, comments, competitors’ ads) and propose what to test next, with a cost and an expected impact.

2. Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: it reads and writes in our whole stack, from ad accounts to CRM, landing pages, webinars, email, social DMs and analytics.

3. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, voice, member stories, past campaigns and what they taught us, in one shared company brain.

4. Ship its hypotheses. Produce the hook, the ad cut, the page, the email sequence or the funnel variant, and put it live.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Every test is tied to a number that matters (cost per qualified lead, show-up rate, close rate, CAC, payback, LTV), not to likes or reach.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Scale what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and launch the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment, with its result. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars, wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations and audit our tracking after every live. Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

We believe self-help is broken: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

We believe people deserve more. More than content. More than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

Growth at Paradox means bringing people into something that actually changes their life, and proving it.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. A small growth team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

We hold ourselves to the standards of Apple, Spotify and MasterClass. While others hide their numbers, we let our results speak.

As Head of Growth, you’ll be the architect of a growth machine that learns by itself. In a classic growth team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls.

You don’t just manage a team, you get your hands dirty to identify growth levers and pull them yourself.

❌ The old way: a Head of Growth hires people and agencies, briefs them, and checks their work.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: your team is mostly agents, and you build them yourself. You write their skills, plug them into our tools (ad accounts, CRM, webinars, email, analytics) and write the evals that tell an agent what "good" means: a hook that stops the scroll, a page that converts, a test that’s worth its cost.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. You move each agent up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month you build the agent that reviews every ad set overnight. At first it only suggests. By month three it pauses losers and moves budget by itself within limits you set, and you only see what was decided and why.

❌ The old way: a media buyer checks the dashboards each morning, changes bids by hand and waits for the weekly report.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build and improve the agents that watch every ad set, page, webinar and email sequence around the clock. They spot what’s dropping, find the cause, fix it or launch a variant, and report what they did.

You give autonomy step by step: an agent can pause an ad on its own long before it can raise a budget. You decide where each agent stops and a human takes over.

Example: show-up rate drops on Tuesday’s webinar. Before anyone notices, an agent has found the cause (a reminder that never went out), fixed it and started testing a new reminder sequence.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an agency, waits two weeks and gets five ads.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every live, interview and member story into hooks, ad cuts and shorts, that study what the best advertisers in the world are running, and that write in our voice.

Your taste becomes the eval: you write down what makes a great hook, a great cut and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Example: one webinar becomes dozens of hook variants by the next morning. You keep the best few, and next week the agents’ first drafts are already closer to your bar.

❌ The old way: an analyst rebuilds the numbers in a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody trusts them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: you build the agents that connect every touch to cash, from ad to lead, live, sale and transformed member, and that audit the tracking after every campaign.

No number, no test: you define the KPIs that matter (CAC, show-up, close rate, LTV, payback), and agents refuse to launch a test that isn’t wired to one.

Example: after each live, an agent cross-checks the ad platform, the CRM and our analytics, finds where they disagree and fixes the leak before the next live.

❌ The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: every result goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before proposing anything. The machine never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: tests per week, time from idea to live, the share of the work done without a human, and how many agents moved up an autonomy level this month. You push those numbers up.

And always: no fake scarcity, no fake claims, real stories told with consent. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. Most experiments are generated and shipped by agents. The output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

You’ll own these KPIs: LTV / CAC ratio, funnel conversion rate by stage, experiments launched per week.

Desired outcomes: conversion rates 2x better than baseline, growth experimentation that runs continuously, a predictable growth engine.

Tools you’ll master: GA4, Optibase, Vidalytics, Clarity, Hotjar, Amplitude, Figma, Looker Studio, VWO, Optimizely, Claude Code, and whatever helps you see what others miss.

You have ideally a minimum of 3 years of experience in Conversion Rate Optimization, Growth Experimentation, or Performance Marketing, ideally within SaaS, e-commerce, or digital product environments.

You have a track record of designing, implementing, and scaling A/B and multivariate tests that have generated measurable improvements in revenue, user activation, or conversion.

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, automations, APIs, evals, creative pipelines. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

A Conversion Architect, skilled at diagnosing funnel bottlenecks and translating data into structured experimentation roadmaps that deliver measurable business outcomes.

A Behavioral Strategist, fascinated by what drives human action, able to mix cognitive psychology, UX, and persuasion frameworks to design experiences that convert because they resonate.

A Data-driven Creator, fluent in GA4, Hotjar, AB Tasty, VWO, Amplitude, not just running tests, but connecting insights to revenue, retention, and lifetime value.

Loop thinker: you see every channel as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

Taste: you know a great hook in two seconds, and you’d rather ship one great ad than ten average ones.

Numbers-honest: you say when a test failed. You care about real revenue, not vanity metrics.

Builder, not manager: you’d rather build the system than coordinate an agency.

Growth over ego: you love hard feedback, you read, study and learn fast.

Cross-disciplinary: you move from psychology to spreadsheet to creative brief to code.

Analytical: you turn a vague problem into a clear hypothesis, a metric and an experiment.

English: Fluent English, you can hold a conversation on specific details with English-speaking colleagues.

Our chosen language for written communication is English.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos — wrong place.

❌ If you prefer balance over intensity — wrong season.

❌ If you build to deliver, not to elevate — wrong mission.

❌ If you want to optimize without getting your hands dirty in landing pages, copy, or design — wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage agencies and budgets rather than build a machine — wrong role.

✅ If you’re obsessed with psychology, systems and creative…

✅ If you want to build the growth machine that learns faster than anyone else…

✅ If you dream of using AI to bring transformation to millions of people…

Build one of the first truly self-learning growth machines, and write the playbook others will copy.

Grow something that genuinely changes lives, with the proof to show it.

Work with filmmakers, psychologists, engineers, and creatives obsessed with impact.

You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

-> 🏅 Being surrounded by A-players who strive to perform

But you don’t just want a competitive paycheck, you also want…

-> Playing a crucial role in the development of a company whose content and products will be used by millions of people

-> Waking up in the morning feeling like you are making a difference is something!

We receive messages every day from clients who have successfully lost weight, quit smoking, reached a milestone in their business, built better relationships, and made positive changes in their lives.

But that’s not all, we believe that driven people deserve…

-> 😎 A company culture based on autonomy & accountability

-> 🗽 Flexible working hours (but very hard working)

-> 🌎 A remote culture from day one with the freedom to work from wherever you want

-> 🧍‍♀️ Standing desks paid by the company if you live far from our offices

-> 🏀 350€ per year paid by the company to take care of yourself (for your gym, or dance class or whatever make you feel great)

-> 📚 150€ of books or education - At Paradox, we are learner lovers and we want to support you in that

-> 🔥 Access to Athena - our Paradox AI Coach supporting you on anything personal or professional, we want you to live your best life

If all this sounds good to you, come and write the next chapter with us 🚀🚀🚀🚀

#7408. Inside Nike’s plan to turn creators into brand builders

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Nike’s turnaround plan gives creators a bigger role in building the brand.

That became clear at its investor day earlier this week (November 17), where execs spent plenty of time on getting closer to athletes and stretched the definition of one to take in far more people.

“When we say athlete, we mean athlete in the broadest sense possible. If you have a body, you are an athlete,” CEO Eliott Hill told investors.

That definition puts everyday participants alongside elite competitors. Separately, Nike is looking to bring creators, cultural voices and local communities closer to the brand as part of its growth strategy. Together, these moves are part of “Full Stride Ahead”, the strategy Nike is counting on to get back to sustainable, profitable growth. Doing so rests on three pillars the brand’s top brass call “Epic”, “Connected” and “Fast.”. They cover product innovation, consumer relationships and speed to market. Connected is where creates and athletes come in.

Brand president Amy Montagne said Nike is changing how it spends its marketing dollars, with more weight on “grassroots connections, local creators and relevant cultural voices”. She said the goal is to reach communities through “the right people, in the right places and at the right moments”.

The splashy camopaigns and superstar deals will carry on. Next to them, Nike is building a looser network of local influence, informed by research across more than 10 markets where it quizzed athletes, retailers, creators and tastemakers about how tastes shift from place to place. Local teams will get more say over product creation, assortments and how the brand shows up in each market. The shift is particularly clear in China, where Nike acknowledged that its previous model was too centralized toward its headquarters in Beaverton, Oregon. It is now moving towards more locally driven product creation and giving teams closer to shoppers greater influence over decisions.

That means changes for creators too. Nike wants their help reading an audience and translating product into local culture, a job that starts well before any campaign lives. The company plans to expand its use of creator networks, entertainment partnerships and athlete-led storytelling.

“Putting the onus on the regions is the right way of thinking as long as it goes deep enough,” said James Kirkham, founder of brand consultancy Iconic. “With JD Sports we worked on ‘second cities’ like Liverpool, Rotterdam and Lyon (these are often port cities for various historic cultural hot spot reasons), which tend to get to culture first.”

The trends in Liverpool are very different from those just 30 miles down the road in Manchester, he continued, right down to the shape and fit of some jogging bottoms and a brand that lumps the two together has already lost so knowing that sheer nuance and level of detail and flagging it is key to proper authentic hits.

Nike’s football plans show this is supposed to work. The company built the strategy around a consumer archetype it calls the “Mad Baller” — a football obsessed teenager who plays the game, watches it and dresses the part. Nike is chasing that kid across what it calls pitch, stands and street which ties elite players and performance boots to fan culture, fashion, creators and grassroots football.

Some of that was on display over the summer. At the World Cup, its “Rip the Script” campaign got 5.7 billion content views, according to the investor day presentation. During it, execs also highlighted how a new generation of creators brought its sotires into football culture generating hundreds of millions of views. Nike then spread the story across individual athlete content, creator collabs, product drops, retail experiences and local tournaments.

Sponsored athletes are in a line for a similar rethink. A player’s value to Nike used to rest mostly on performance and visibility during tournaments. Now, the company wants their training, persona;lities and fan relationships to keep the brand in people’s feeds all year. That also opens up collaborations with creators that reach well beyond the athletes own sport.

Nike seems more interested in bringing creators into how the brand gets built than in buying sponsored posts. A running creator may understand amateur runners better than an ad agency does. A local fashion figure in Lagos or Seoul may have a sharper sense of how sport feeds into streetwear in their city than a global marketing team in Beaverton. People like that can spot trends early, shape storytelling and turn passing interest into actual participation.

Plenty of that work will still look like ordinary campaign and content distribution, of course, and most creators won’t become long-term strategic partners. Yet Nike’s growing use of creator networks could become a competitive advantage, particularly when creators have real standing in a sporting or cultural community. For these creators, the opportunity could extend beyond sponsored content into partnerships involving product, communities and intellectual property.

Nike offered another example of this approach through its Studio Fleece launch. The initiative brought together more than 100 athletes, entertainers, creators and stylists, combining product seeding, creative participation and Nike By You experiences. The collection was distributed across more than 6,000 retail outlets. Nike described it as the largest new apparel launch in its history, with the highest proportion of sales made at full price of any fleece launch.

None of this matters if it doesn’t contribute to sales so Nike is focusing on five priority sports: running, training, global football, basketball and outdoor plus sportswear. More often than not those efforts will be focused on 23 countries that Nike expects to deliver about 90% of its growth opportunity between fiscal 2028 and 2031. The goal is around $5 billion in extra revenue by fiscal 2031, compared with fiscal 2027. Nike expeccts about $4 billion of that to come from the five sports and about one billion to come from sportswear.

Running was the proof point the investor day presentations kept coming back to. The category has delivered nine consecutive quarters of growth from the first quarter of its fiscal year in 2025 through to the first for 2027, including six straight quarters of double-digit growth. That’s around £1 billion revenue for the business.

Nike’s Montagne described the wider goal as building “a repeatable engine to create demand,” and that’s the phrase investors are likely to hold the company to. Indeed, Nike wants a model it can roll out across sports and markets deliberately so it isn’t waiting for the occasional cultural moment to land.

A bigger role in a brand’s strategy raises new questions for creators, athletes and the people who represent them. A creator who helps a brand understand a community and build demand there might reasonably look for something beyond a campaign fee. An athlete who helps build a content platform or a product line could push to share in the long-term value they help create. And as brand-run realtor networks get more sophisticated, ownership of the audience, the content and the IP becomes a bigger part of any negotiation.

Investor Days are always about a company’s growth plans so no surprise that compensation models weren’t on the agenda. Those conversations are more likely to happen deal by deal, and in turn create another boon for the cottage industry of agencies, talent managers and creator businesses that have emerged around the creator economy to put together partnerships that account for the wider value creators and athletes bring.

“If I were managing a creator right now then I’d tell them to play up everything specific about themselves: their expertise, their obsessions, their accent, their postcode. Get into the nerdiest detail you’ve got and bring every bit of it to life,” said Iconic’s Kirkham “The deals Nike is describing will go to deep experts with a real local standing and a million views on last decade’s platforms won’t get anyone really bothered or doing the marketing for you any more, you need to go beautifully deep and nuanced.”

Nike has the scale, product and the distribution. The cultural pull and local knowledge it wants increasingly sit outside the company, with creators and athletes it’s now trying to bring closer to the centre of its business.

#7405. In Graphic Detail: Welcome to the agentic commerce quarter

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Holiday 2026 is shaping up as the first season where the shopping agent actually pays — solidifying Q4 as the agentic commerce quarter.

OpenAI launched ChatGPT wallet this week to push agent purchase through retailer checkouts, alongside a commerce partner playbook aimed at helping businesses prepare for a future of more agent-led purchase. Google began rolling its UCP-powered checkout in Canada through AI Mode and Gemini. Amazon blocked Meta’s Muse AI agent from its retail site. Walmart lets customers cap an agent’s spending at $50, $100 or $200. And Bain reckons roughly one in four online shoppers plan to start their holiday shopping journeys on an AI platform.

Put together, agentic commerce is having a growth spurt even if it’s still a while from growing up.

Agentic commerce is moving from hypothetical to commercial opportunity, with platforms and retailers beginning to position themselves for a new way of shopping. U.S. retail e-commerce sales completed through end-to-end agentic commerce are forecast to reach $1.44 billion this year, according to eMarketer. That figure is expected to increase 10.6% to $1.59 billion in 2027, a further 11% increase to $1.76 billion in 2028 and reach $2.17 trillion by 2030.

Marketers are already adapting their strategies to account for AI agents, although the focus so far seems to be making brands discoverable, rather than enabling agents to buy from them.

According to Gartner’s 2026 Marketing Strategy and Engagement Survey, 61% of senior marketing leaders have adapted content and SEO/AEO to improve machine interpretability. A further 42% are monitoring and correcting misinformation about their brands in AI-generated outputs, while 39% have tailored product information, pricing or offers for machine and agent decision-making.

But the same survey found that fewer have taken proper steps yet to accommodate agent-led transactions. Around a third (30%) of marketers have designed interfaces or workflows for agent transactions, while 32% manage machines and agents as a distinct audience alongside human customers.

Brands might be getting ready for agents, but most consumers aren’t yet comfortable letting one spend their money unsupervised.

According to U.S. identity and access management company Okta’s Customer Identity Trends Report 2026, when consumers were asked how likely they’d be to let an AI agent complete a purchase without reviewing it first, only a third (31%) said they were very or rather likely to. At the same time, 67% of those surveyed said they were unlikely to let an AI agent finish a purchase without review, while 41% said they wouldn’t at all.

Of course, there are exceptions. Early adopters of the tech are far more trusting. Nearly two thirds (62%) said they’d let an AI agent complete a purchase without checking, and 42% said they’d be very likely to.

The gap suggests the hard part of agentic commerce may not be getting the right tech, but instead building the right amount of trust.

Shoppers might not be handing over full control to the checkout just yet, but AI tools are already influencing where they go to buy, and the visitors those tools send are more likely to spend.

In September, shoppers who arrived at U.S. retail e-commerce sites from AI tools converted 90% better than those from other channels, according to Adobe Digital Insights. That’s the highest level Adobe has recorded, and a sharp turnaround from August 2025 when AI traffic converted 9% worse than other channels.

For major e-commerce businesses, agentic commerce could have implications beyond how products are sold. It could also change how retailers make money from helping shoppers to discover them.

Amazon, for example, generated $68.5 billion in net sales from its online stores during Q3 2026, alongside $46.5 billion from third-party seller services, and $20 billion from ad services, according to Enders Analysis.

As agentic commerce becomes a more natural part of the consumer journey, shoppers may increasingly rely on agents to identify and recommend products rather than browse sponsored listings. It could affect Amazon’s business in different ways: its fulfilment infrastructure could support transactions initiated elsewhere, while its advertising business could face pressure if fewer shoppers encounter paid placements on its platform.

#7404. How Celsius doubled down on sports marketing campaigns in 2026

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

Energy drink brand Celsius is rapidly expanding its footprint across the sports landscape.

The company is increasingly leveraging partnerships with major sports leagues, college athletics and high-profile athletic events to connect with health-conscious consumers. In 2026 alone, Celsius has announced several sponsorships and marketing activations across professional and amateur sporting events.

In August, Celsius also became the official energy drink sponsor of ESPN’s College GameDay. The multi-year partnership kicked off for the 2026 season at major campus stops, with activations including run clubs and tailgates. For its college football NIL (name, image and likeness) integration, Celsius partnered with college football analyst Kirk Herbstreit, alongside a collegiate roster that included Michigan quarterback Bryce Underwood and Oregon quarterback Dante Moore. To close out the year, Celsius is delving deeper into the burgeoning world of Formula 1. The energy drink brand signed British professional driver Jessica Hawkins ahead of the 2026 Las Vegas Grand Prix this month. By aligning its brand with active lifestyles and high-performance athletics, Celsius aims to solidify its position as a better-for-you functional beverage for elite competitors and everyday fitness enthusiasts.

Many professional sporting events are sponsored by major hydration or alcohol brands. However, Celsius says its origins as a better-for-you alternative to sugary energy drinks position it as a product that can support fitness enthusiasts and athletes.

Running has also quickly become a focus of Celsius’ sports marketing strategy. Most recently, the brand returned with a Club Celsius pop-up at the New York City Marathon, which included a multi-day takeover in Brooklyn along the race route. Throughout the fall, the brand also sponsored Diplo’s Run Club stops in New York, Chicago and Los Angeles. In a related category, fitness events and competitions are a growing marketing opportunity for Celsius. Earlier this year, the brand also became the official global energy drink partner of the fitness competition ATHX Games.

Kyle Watson, CMO of Celsius, told Modern Retail that with the ongoing Live. Fit. Go. campaign at the center of the brand, sports is a natural and effective space for Celsius to show up. The 360 campaign, which targets active consumers, launched in 2025 with TV, paid ads, organic social, out-of-home and experiential activations.

“What differentiates us is the breadth of our presence,” Watson said. “Celsius isn’t defined by a single sport; we’re building meaningful connections across the entire sports landscape.”

That has meant heavy investment across collegiate sports for the past few years, largely through strategic NIL partnerships with athletes across football, basketball and gymnastics. “This year, we took that presence to the next level through our multi-year partnership with ESPN’s College GameDay,” said Watson. Through Celsius Combine, a football-inspired fan event, along with campus run clubs, Watson said the brand is offering immersive experiences that embody the Live. Fit. Go. campaign.

More and more, brands are using sporting events to do official or proximity marketing to capture fan excitement. This has become a popular tactic during big events like the Super Bowl and even college sports tournaments.

Anjali Bal, associate professor of marketing at Babson College, said today’s consumers are highly invested in sporting events, which makes them prime moments for marketing campaigns.

“Adrenaline and psychological responses are high” at these events, Bal said. “When you tie this to team loyalty and the desire to belong, consumers are more open to messaging and brands; they align with their values and their teams.”

That said, Watson said Celsius views its sports strategy as extending beyond traditional sporting events. For example, Watson said, “Through our multi-year partnership with Aston Martin Aramco, one of the most ambitious teams in Formula 1, we’re showing up at key races and connecting with a premium, global audience.” Watson added that Celsius’ Formula 1 presence is another example of how the brand is trying to credibly align with elite athletes and competitors across various disciplines. “Because our product is a drink for athletes, regardless of the sport,” Watson added.

Looking ahead, Watson said Celsius will continue to evolve its sports strategy to reach people more often and in more meaningful ways. “In 2027, consumers can expect even more impactful partnerships with leading teams, elite athletes and high-performance programs, all designed to bring the Live. Fit. Go. mentality to life,” Watson said.

#7402. Mail Metro Media’s next creator play is representing them

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Mail Metro Media now thinks “more like a talent agency” and commissions “like a broadcaster”, according to Hannah Blake, its managing director of creator and new media. Its creators have, in other words, become the business.

Audiences tune in for them, brands pay to reach those audiences, and the deals have grown from around £5,000 apiece to worth seven figures in October. Holding on to those creators is now as important as finding them since any one who leaves for an outside agent can take the audience and advertisers along too.

Blake spelled out that risk on stage at the Digiday Media Strategies London event in October.

“The huge risk is that we make these people super famous and then they go and get signed by a different agent,” she said.

So in September the ad sales arm for the Daily Mail hired David White from Whalar as head of creators, with a brief to build an in-house talent agency that represents its creators and signs outside talent too. White was the first hire for the company’s Stars business in New York, the talent management arm that handles creators’ careers beyond brand deals and was also a senior talent manager there.

What the business ultimately ends up with is still open. Blake described something that “potentially could look more like a talent agency and an employer,” and she hopes to have it in market by the end of the year. The final shape matters because running a talent agency would be a big change of job for a newspaper group’s ad sales team. It exists to sell audiences to brands while an agent’s job is to get the best deal for the talent. Mail Metro Media would be trying to do both at once.

That’s the potted history of how Mail Metro Media reached this point. The full story is a lot more nuanced.

It started four years ago when Daily Mail launched Eliza, a social-only brand for young women fronted by six fashion and beauty creators. Its hit format, “Guess Which Outfit’s Most Expensive”, puts two women on the street, one in a £10,000 outfit and the other in a high-street copy, and leaves viewers to guess which is which. It kept going viral, people kept coming back and advertisers soon followed. That’s despite it being relatively small next to the rest of the Mail’s portfolio, and yet “we were doing lots of direct advertisers relationships quite quickly”, Blake said. Fast forward to now,  and brands often book content on Eliza and then want its creators posting on their own accounts too, which Mail Metro Media packages into the deal.

The Mail’s own TikTok was a different story. The Mail and Metro launched on the platform around the same time as Eliza and the former became the biggest news publisher there, with 20 million followers, on the back of rapid-fire news clips. But the money didn’t follow.  “The huge issue was there was no monetization there,” Blake said, even though publishers had widely expected TikTok to start paying them for views. Advertisers weren’t keen either, Every rival was posting the same kind of clips and the Daily Mail couldn’t build a brand into footage it had brought in from elsewhere.

Together, they showed the publisher that the money was in shows it owned and creators audiences came back for. It shifted course accordingly, and over the past year it relaunched the Mail and Metro on social around repeatable shows it owns, such as the sports debate show “Dugout Debate” with creators at the front and helping shape them. Blake said the operation now pulls 5 billion monthly video views across 10 brands and signed its first seven-figure deal last month. With that success comes a new kind of risk. The creators fronting those shows have built followings of their own and “now some of the superstars are beginning to emerge,” Blake said. Left unchecked, the publisher ends up making stars for outside agencies to sign.

In some ways, the publisher has already been acting like an agent of sorts. Alongside its core bundle where brands pay one flat rate for a run of content with “no differentiation between the talent,” Blake said it has spent the past year selling individual creators on their own channels. It calls this add-on “Creator Amp.” One example is Mimi Yates, a young journalist who fronts much of the news output and has built a following of her own. Brands can book her as part of a wider campaign or on her own, and on their personal accounts the creators “all set their own rates,” Blake said.

That arrangement was easy to manage while the talent was still unknown.

“When we started, our creators were all emerging,” Blake said. With bigger names now coming through, the publisher wants something sturdier. “We are now at the point where we’re beginning to formalize that relationship a bit more,” she said, and hiring White was the first step.

He will recruit from two places. One is from its own staff. The other is outside talent Blake described as “the next generation of emerging news and entertainment talent”.

Moves like this bring some awkward conversations with them. Daily Mail’s creators are salaried and Nathan Aspell, director of the New Media division, said the company owns the IP they develop. Blake described those same creators setting their own rates on their own channels. How the money is split between the employer and agent will go a long way to deciding whether a breakout star sticks around.

Axel Springer’s version hands journalists a share of the financial results in return for production, marketing and distribution. Daily Mail hasn’t said what its agency will charge.

Then there’s the small matter of journalism. The same person can read the headlines and sell the mascara, and the publisher would be both their employer and their agent. That’s a lot of hats for one newsroom.

White’s first signings will show how far the publisher plans to take this venture. Blake thinks publishers are headed this way regardless. “I actually think it’s just a byproduct of where the opportunity is,” she said.

For a lot of publishers, signing talent still feels like someone else’s business and talk of newsrooms turning into agencies tends to raise eyebrows. Blake sees it as following the money, which increasingly sits with “people-centric IP that exists across multiple different touchpoints”.

Plenty of rivals are following it too.  Axel Springer launched its own spin on the same idea last month, while Hearst did so earlier this week. Increasingly, publishers see a talent operation as the next step in their latest pivot to video, which runs on personalities whose audiences, and the ad money chasing them, follow them wherever they go.

“Publishers are responding to the audience’s desire for personality-led formats while limiting strategic dependence on individual journalists and managing talent,” said Laura Darcey, research analyst at Enders Analysis.

Editor’s note: An earlier version of this article said David White ran Whalar’s Stars business in New York. He was the first hire for the division in New York.

#7398. Last of the old school buyers, Publicis Media vet John Muszynski prepares to retire

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

He’s equally comfortable at the negotiating table or on skates in an ice rink.

He doesn’t do it himself but he knows principal media is part of the business.

He’s said to have once humbled the CEO of a major cable network group by holding back $200 million of his clients’ media spend when that CEO tried to bully him into big price increases.

He’s the longest-serving head of investment in media. And now, John Muszynski, legendary media investment executive with Publicis Media, plans to retire.

After 45 years at one holding company and negotiating nearly as many upfronts over that span, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Chicago-based Muszynski, who oversees decision-making on what Publicis estimates to be 40% of the U.S. video marketplace inventory, is arguably the last of the old-school heads of investment that have seen the upfronts morph into the byzantine bazaar it is today.

“For nearly five decades, I’ve had a front row seat to the industry’s transformation,” Muszynski told Digiday. “It’s gone from three networks to always-on content spanning the entire digital ecosystem. It’s been fascinating to be a part of it all.”

The avid ice hockey fan, hunter and fisherman (when he’s not negotiating) got his start in 1981 as a planner for Leo Burnett’s burgeoning media unit, handling longtime Publicis clients including Kimberly-Clark, McDonald’s and Kellogg’s. That unit eventually became standalone media agency Starcom, for which he rose to CEO of Starcom USA from 2005-2009. He then helped to build Spark Foundry from a 65-person boutique into a 3,000-person media agency.

In 2016, Publicis formed Publicis Media Exchange, which harnessed the collective buying clout of what was then Starcom, Mediavest, Spark, Zenith, Optimedia and Blue 449 media agencies. He was the lead negotiator when that formed, and became chairman of the group in 2022.

The execs who sat across from him at the negotiating table said they always felt they got a fair shake — in part because Muszynski always came prepared, was knowledgable and true to his word.

“John has been a tremendous influence on generations of marketers, agency team members and publishers,” said Mark Marshall, chairman of global advertising and partnerships at NBCUniversal. “He has always put his clients’ objectives at the forefront and pushed all of us to deliver for those clients. He is the epitome of ‘a handshake is his word’.”

Rita Ferro, president, global advertising, disney, was even more personal in her assessment of working with Muszynski. “John is a legend in this business and from the moment we met, he became a trusted partner and more importantly a dear friend,” she said.

Sean Moran, COO at Evergreen Trading agency, who negotiated with Muszynski during his 24 years as an ad sales exec with Viacom until 2020, thinks of Muszynski as a contemporary of the old-school buyers of the TV business, from Grey Advertising’s Jon Mandel to UM’s Donna Wolfe, and from Omnicom’s John Swift to GroupM’s Rino Scanzoni.

“When I was running MTV and we had a big upfront event, and had to get people there, if I could get John Muszynski to fly in and come to my event, that was a huge win,” said Moran. ”He represented so much gravitas. Obviously he controlled a lot of budgets, but it was also because he was so smart and yet had such empathy for good people. You couldn’t bullshit him. He was a good Midwestern guy who could talk about a great deal over over a steak, but he did his homework and knew his numbers.”

Moran said Muszynski often set the marketplace as “the main controller” of the upfronts. “One of his negotiating tactics was, ‘Yeah, I haven’t seen everything, but boy, it looks down’,” said Moran. “If he respected your inventory, you could do a deal with him fairly quickly and cut through the chaff. He was known to pull that with one or two people that were at the front of the line, and protect his clients. He would therefore set the marketplace for himself. It was very smart.”

Although Muszynski will retire from his full-time position at PMX, he will stay on to consult through 2027, working with Joel Lunenfeld who leads PMX globally and Mark Griffin, who oversees all investment in the U.S. as chief investment officer.

Mediaplus and Publicis clients, including Banco Santander, have paused TikTok spending in wake of investigations into abuse images circulating on the video platform.

Madison and Wall estimates creator marketing growing at double the pace of the rest of advertising, driven on by creator-led production investments.

A new crop of companies promise to coordinate advertising budgets across platforms, but can they control buying decisions, or merely add another interface to the existing system?

#7395. Correcting the TV ad market’s imperfect shift to streaming

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Evan Rutchik, president and Chief Strategy Officer, Viamedia.ai

The TV ad industry’s shift from linear to streaming is real, but the financial truth underlying that shift may not align with many people’s perception of the transition. That’s because the movement of ad budgets from linear to streaming has hardly been evenly paced.

Streaming’s share of ad revenue is indeed growing at major media companies, including The Walt Disney Company, Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery. But their earnings tell a different story: Linear revenue is falling faster than streaming revenue is rising. The shift is happening, but it’s not benefiting content owners as expected.

The core problem is a supply-demand imbalance. Streaming ad revenue is growing through increased volume, not higher prices. New inventory floods in, making cheap inventory abundant. As major subscription services continue to promote free ad-supported streaming tiers, the buyers’ market will only continue.

The solution is to grow demand, not increase prices. Fortunately, the ad dollars needed to support the streaming market already exist, sitting in local and midmarket budgets that haven’t been able to buy national CTV efficiently. The opportunity is to bring these advertisers into the broader video and streaming market, then develop ways to keep them investing.

Correcting the imbalance requires creating new demand, then ensuring that content quality and product performance are strong enough to eventually shift pricing dynamics.

When it comes to growth, the glut of cheap national SVOD supply may be a problem for programmers, but it’s an opportunity for local and midmarket advertisers. If the current streaming market is largely aimed at Fortune 500 companies, the industry has to consider the thousands of smaller advertisers that make steady annual investments in local linear buys. Taken collectively, these advertisers represent billions of dollars in ad budgets.

Because these advertisers have smaller budgets, many haven’t tried streaming. It is more cost-efficient for them to execute local and spot buys on linear than to invest in targeted or national streaming campaigns. These advertisers are still seeing real success reaching millions of linear viewers, so they understand the power of TV. They just need an entry point for CTV. Solving this issue would allow demand to flow steadily into the market.

Once that demand enters the market, additional steps must happen. CPMs fall when inventory is sold as undifferentiated tonnage. With platforms like Disney+ and Netflix exploring free ad-supported tiers, even more low-cost supply will enter the market. But cheap supply that doesn’t perform isn’t worth an advertiser’s investment. If that inventory doesn’t deliver results for advertisers outside the Fortune 500, those advertisers will invest their budgets elsewhere, creating another demand imbalance.

The answer lies in household addressability. While this is available on some streaming platforms, it’s still largely absent from the overall TV market, including linear. Household-level addressability turns cheap impressions into targeted impressions worth buying, even within highly premium inventory. The more information brands have about who will see their ads and whether those ads are likely to resonate with the viewer, the more they are likely to pay.

It is no secret that streaming is the future of TV, and shifting revenue numbers reflect that. To build a sustainable future for media companies and advertisers, the entire ecosystem must first provide ease of access and then addressability.

Making streaming easier to buy at scale would help solve the demand problem. Once more advertisers are in the market, addressability creates greater competition for impressions, solving the pricing imbalance.

This future is out there, provided the industry takes a few steps in the right direction.

After 45 years of negotiating, the chairman of Publicis Media Exchange (PMX) U.S., is stepping down from a full-time role this year, although he plans to continue to consult with the holdco.

Why the Daily Mail’s ad sales team is turning talent agent.

The company is increasingly leveraging partnerships with major sports leagues, college athletics and high-profile athletic events.

Own the packaging: titles, thumbnails and the first 3 seconds. You test variants every week and know your click-through and retention numbers by heart.

❌ The old way: one shoot becomes one video and a handful of posts.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: one shoot, one live or one interview becomes dozens of hooks, shorts, carousels and ad cuts by the next morning, in both languages. Concepts are tested with AI-generated drafts before we spend a day shooting them.

Your taste becomes the eval. You write down what makes a great hook, a great cut, a great carousel and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Act as the final quality check: nothing gets published if it doesn’t meet the "Paradox Standard" of aesthetics and storytelling, whether a person or an agent made it.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an editor, waits three days and gets two versions.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents draft, cut, caption, translate and schedule. You keep the best, and next week their first drafts are already closer to your bar.

Analyze retention graphs, CTR, and CPL to iterate based on feedback, killing what fails and aggressively scaling what works.

❌ The old way: someone pulls the stats at the end of the month and nobody changes anything.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents read the numbers of every post every day, explain why it worked or didn’t, and propose the next test. You measure the machine itself: posts per week, time from idea to live, hit rate, and the share of the work done without a human.

We started in French. You build the machine that takes Paradox to English-speaking audiences: channels, formats and voice that feel native, not translated.

And always: real stories told with consent, no fake claims, no fake scarcity. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time on concepts, taste and calls, not on resizing, captioning and exporting. The output grows every month while the team doesn’t.

Proven Content Builder: You have successfully grown a media brand, a YouTube channel, or a personal brand to significant scale (100k+ followers or millions of views). You understand the mechanics of virality and retention. You’ve created at least one format that broke out, and you can show its click-through and retention numbers.

Elite Scripting Skills: You can show us scripts you wrote that performed. You know how to structure a narrative, write a hook that stops the scroll, and keep an audience engaged for 20+ minutes.

Performance Fluency: You have worked in environments where content had to drive business results (ads, launches, conversion), not just vanity metrics.

AI-native Creative: you’ve built content pipelines with AI yourself: Claude Code, automations, generative video, image and voice tools, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Intolerant to Mediocrity: You have "Taste." You are physically pained by a bad font, a clumsy cut, or a weak headline. You fight for the details that others ignore, including in what AI produces.

Psychology Obsessed: You don’t just "make content"; you study human nature. You constantly ask why people click, why they share, and what emotion drives them.

A "Doer" with Authority: You command respect not by title, but by competence. You can jump into a project, write the script, direct the shoot, and fix the edit if needed.

Loop thinker: you see every platform as a loop that can learn, and you can’t stop yourself from closing it.

No Ego, Just Impact: You don’t fall in love with your ideas. You accept that even the best concepts can flop. When it happens, you don’t take it personally; you analyze the data, pivot immediately, and ship the next concept with renewed energy.

English: Fluent English, you can hold a conversation on specific details with English-speaking colleagues.

Our chosen language for written communication is English.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos: wrong place.

❌ If you don’t genuinely care about the topics we cover: wrong place.

❌ If you want to strategize without getting your hands dirty in scripting, directing, or the editing suite: wrong role.

#7357. Newsmax Sues Fox Over Roku Deal, Alleging Antitrust Violation

Newsmax is suing Fox Corp., alleging its recently completed $22 billion purchase of Roku unlawfully consolidates control over America’s rapidly growing free streaming television market.

The antitrust lawsuit was filed Wednesday in the United States District Court for the Northern District of California. In a shared statement, Newsmax said Fox owning Tubi and The Roku Channel, the nation’s two largest FAST platforms, gives it approximately 71% of the U.S. FAST television market as measured by viewing time.

Newsmax is seeking injunctive relief to stop further integration of the companies, preserve Roku as an independently managed business, and ultimately require Fox to divest either Tubi or The Roku Channel.

Newsmax founder and CEO Christopher Ruddy said in a statement, “This merger represents an extraordinary threat to competition, consumer choice, and the ability of independent news organizations to reach the American public.”

For its part, Fox Corp., in a statement shared with TVNewser, said, “Newsmax’s complaint, which comes after a rigorous review process by regulatory agencies, is wholly without merit. Upon closing, we commenced actively integrating this approved transaction for the benefit of consumers, partners, and other stakeholders.”

Earlier this month, the Rupert Murdoch-owned company completed its purchase, combining Fox Corp.’s broadcast, news, sports, streaming, and local news portfolio with Roku’s open connected TV platform and first-party data from more than 100 million global streaming households.

As part of its agreement with the Department of Justice to take over Roku, Fox Corp. committed to keeping Roku open and neutral for at least three years, including continuing to carry non-Fox content, avoiding search or placement discrimination, providing third-party data products, and maintaining contractual firewalls.

This is not the first time Newsmax has tussled with Fox; in Sept. 2025, a Florida judge dismissed another Newsmax lawsuit against Fox News without prejudice. That lawsuit alleged the Rupert Murdoch-owned network engaged in an extensive, unlawful campaign to block competition in the right-wing pay-TV market.

Fox News responded to the dismissed lawsuit by saying, in a statement to TVNewser: “Newsmax cannot sue its way out of its own competitive failures in the marketplace to chase headlines simply because it can’t attract viewers.”

Mark Mwachiro is a TVNewser contributor who has been writing for the blog since March 2022. Based out of New York, Mark has also contributed to ADWEEK.

#7352. Elon Musk: ‘I Feel Pretty Foolish’ Spending Record $445 Million on Midterm Elections

Elon Musk regrets spending so much money during the U.S. midterm elections.

The billionaire SpaceX and Tesla CEO shelled out at least $445 million in support of Republican candidates and conservative PACs, according to data monitored by campaign finance watchdog OpenSecrets.

He said his “motivations were very simple” in the decision to spend that sum, “which is just to sort of to have… either the House, Senate, or presidency be Republican in ‘29, and not have… in the worst case scenario, a supermajority of of Democrats.”

#7347. MoviePass Explores a Sale, Pitching Streamers on a Theatrical Bundle

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

MoviePass is exploring a sale and has held talks with several potential buyers, including a number of major streaming companies, according to a person familiar with the sale process.

The talks have been underway for roughly six weeks and have reached the executive level, but no formal bid has been made, according to the person.

At the center of the pitch is a hypothetical product: a “movie theater tier” sold as an add-on to a streaming subscription and facilitated by MoviePass.

The talks come as theatrical attendance is recovering, particularly among Gen Z moviegoers, and as media companies race to build direct relationships with audiences.

Film-adjacent consumer brands have also become hot properties, as evidenced by The New York Times’ recent $330 million bid for Letterboxd, a valuation of more than 20 times its projected 2026 earnings.

No target price has been set, but a valuation based on revenue, rather than earnings, could trade at a multiple of between eight to ten times revenue, the person said.

A sale would also mark another turn for a brand best known for burning through cash.

MoviePass did not provide a comment on the record.

The brand still carries outsize recognition, but acquiring customers at scale as an independent company has proven difficult without raising capital, a reality that prompted the sales process, the person said.

The deck positions MoviePass as the missing piece of a streamer’s subscription, according to the document.

“Every major streamer owns the home screen,” it reads. “MoviePass completes the picture: the theater, brought into the same subscription—one bundle for the film at home and the film in the cinema.”

A studio-owned streamer would pay full price for each ticket, but as the film’s distributor, it would recoup roughly half of that price through its share of the box office. As a result, each redemption of the buyer’s own film would cost it roughly $5, according to the document.

If 1 million members convert, the model would produce between $60 million and $78 million in profit per year; at 20 million members, it would yield between $1.2 billion and nearly $1.6 billion annually, according to the document.

Critically, the $10 price is simply a stand-in. The final figure, as well as the number of ticket visits it covers, could vary, according to the person.

The appeal of the product is strongest for companies that own both a film studio and a streaming service, according to the person.

Streaming companies have shown the most interest, but other types of buyers, including a data-focused company, are also in the mix, according to the person.

The theoretical moat lies in a series of patents that MoviePass holds, including one that lets members pay at any theater that accepts Mastercard without a direct integration with each chain, according to the person. In 2018, MoviePass sued rival Sinemia for patent infringement, and Sinemia shut down its U.S. operations the following year.

The infrastructure could also become a revenue source of its own, licensed to other studio-streamers at $1 per member per month, according to the document.

Any deal remains far from certain. A transaction would require sign-off from many decision-makers at large companies that have not yet decided how to proceed, according to the person.

Hearst Contracts Creators (EXCLUSIVE): On Monday, Hearst Magazines debuted its Hearst Creator Studios, which at launch will connect a group of around 50 editor-selected creators with Hearst editorial brands and advertisers to create original content. Several other publishers, including The Washington Post, Morning Brew, and Daily Mail, have unveiled similar programs, but none have stood up a studio quite like this. It also plans to expand rapidly, tripling its creator roster and getting all of its editorial brands involved by next year. The service reflects one of the most fascinating dynamics in media right now: the evolving question of how creators and media companies will work together, and what each party brings to the other.

A Perfectly Imperfect Platform (EXCLUSIVE): On Monday, I broke the news that Perfectly Imperfect, a former Substack newsletter that evolved into a recommendations platform, is launching an advertising agency. The debut is only the first in a series of forthcoming products, which also include a dating app and newsletter service, connected by the overarching “anti-algorithm” ethos of Perfectly Imperfect. The five-person company, which brought in around $2 million in revenue this year and is pacing for $5 million in 2027, aims to capitalize on the growing prominence of “taste,” but to do so it will have to navigate the challenge that inevitably faces anything cool looking to professionalize: Can its pirate ship spirit scale?

NowThis Adds Impact (EXCLUSIVE): The social publisher NowThis acquired Impact Media on Tuesday in a seven-figure, all-cash deal, NowThis chief executive Sharon Mussalli told me. Across its editorial properties, Impact Media has more than 4 million followers and reaches more than 55 million unique viewers monthly, instantly expanding the audience of politically active, culturally attuned young people that NowThis can offer to its advertisers. NowThis, which brought in around $20 million in revenue and $4.6 million in EBITDA in 2024, was one of the first publishers to embrace socially native television, creating franchises like Are You Okay?, Judgy, and Standup Desk that live entirely on Instagram, TikTok, and YouTube. Now that every other media company has followed suit, NowThis is investing in its growth to preserve its lead, although doing so will result in a planned loss for the year, per Mussalli.

OOH to the Moon: Last month, I wrote that marketers are increasingly turning to out-of-home advertising as the open internet crumbles, trust in social media degrades, and agents replace human shoppers. On Wednesday, the trade agency representing the OOH industry, called OAAA, reported that third-quarter revenue across the segment grew 9.8% to $2.34 billion, marking its strongest third quarter on record. After rebounding from the pandemic, OOH grew 2.1% in 2023, 4.5% in 2024, and 3.6% in 2025, compared to 9.4% growth through the first nine months of 2026—excluding a fourth quarter that is poised to widen that gap even further. Digital out-of-home in particular grew 19% year-over-year and now accounts for 38.1% of total Q3 revenue. As digital media grows more unreliable, watch for spend to migrate to more reliable media.

Alice Ma is the co-founder of Mad Realities, the social video studio behind, among other programs, the viral series Shop Cats, which profiles New York City bodegas and the shop cats that prowl them. The company makes what it describes as television for social media, and in June it joined the roster of Fox Creator Studios, which is now backing new formats like the dating show Love Pitch and the culinary competition Picky Eaters.

As creators and legacy media converge, studios like Mad Realities are carving out a new tier of programming: pricier than a creator with a phone, but cheaper than traditional TV. In our conversation at Web Summit in Lisbon, Ma and I discussed the origins of the company, how it develops shows, and the future of social television.

Mark Stenberg: Where did the idea for Mad Realities come from?

Alice Ma: In 2020, at the peak of Clubhouse, I built a Clubhouse competitor for teenage girls in my dad’s garage. It was a really good app, but no one cared because software alone doesn’t have a moat anymore. So I found my co-founder, who had hosted the No. 1 show on Clubhouse, and we asked: “If people want the show, where do you go?” You either wait years for a TV deal or build an entire media business. So we started the network we would want.

Mark: How does a show go from an idea to a series?

Alice: Development looks similar to TV: We develop ideas internally, and we take outside pitches, often from producers. Sometimes a host comes to us with a topic we really want to work on. The difference is that in TV, development means you aren’t making anything, and a pilot is very expensive. For us, we make the pilot by making the show. If we have conviction in it, we order more episodes, and our deals are structured with options.

Alice: We’re 10 people, plus contractors, and we raised a seed round. We worked with 25 brands in 2025, and so far, the demand has all been inbound, with 60% to 70% coming directly from brands.

Mark: Is the goal to move toward higher production value?

Alice: More resources against a show doesn’t always have to mean higher production value. I think of production value as unlocking a story that couldn’t have been told before. Picky Eaters, the first show we launched with Fox Creator Studios, is a four-camera cooking competition—nobody could make this in their bedroom with a camera. That being said, if another company says it needs five times our budget for the same kind of show, it will lose, because its economics will never be as good.

Mark: Creators and marketers often say that YouTube is more appealing because it is more predictable. Do you agree?

Alice: YouTube’s algorithm is more stable and therefore predictable for large creators. But TikTok and other short-form algorithms enable overnight virality and discovery, which means we can test new formats more quickly.

#7344. Yubico Inc.: Senior Product Manager

At Yubico, we are guided by a shared belief that your work matters. #7341. Brands Must Sharpen Their Storytelling For Agentic Commerce

When I went from being a Disney entertainment executive to Marriott CMO in 2013, I moved quickly to bring more storytelling function into a brand environment. We set up social media hubs around the world, which became a lab for using new tech to accelerate company stories.

The CEO, Arne Sorenson, embraced this move, doing his CNBC interviews from there. Visitors to the headquarters could see how our brand news moved at the speed of culture. Lifting practices and talent from the content business and placing them within a brand was novel to do, and it let social media become visible and repeatable inside a vast organization.

At the time, the marketing industry didn’t have a handle for this structure, and when our team took home a Cannes Gold Lion for the work, the jury assigned it to a new category: Creative Data. The years progressed, and social media practices became common and understood.

Agentic commerce will soon impact marketing the way social media has done — both having started with a sense of unknown until hitting critical mass. Plus, storytelling in the age of agentic commerce has a potential topspin when agents, transacting on behalf of customers, gain insight beyond what the brand may have planned.

Let’s call this Creative Data 2.0, where connected loops help guide internal input and external output.

Leaders from different departments should meet in one shared group to decide how the company uses new technology. They’d weigh what the tech can do against the company’s goals, and the kind of presence the brand wants to have. Companies did something similar when social media was new, with several teams working together to figure out how to tell the brand’s story there.

In this era of AI, marketers influence consumers as well as the agents that act on their behalf. Agentic commerce will eventually convene multiple disciplines to develop a cross-functional playbook in service of coordinated goals. We’ll see transactions optimized because enterprise data presented the product mix to advance the brand story. We’ll see agentic commerce enabling seamless payments and sign on.

But brands must be intentional in their strategy and storytelling in order to be known by the agent. In the accelerated pace of AI, the timing and silos of brand storytelling are collapsing, as agents are tracking brand news and “always on” is the norm.

First, decide what role the brand plays in agentic commerce. From there, it is easier to decide how to assemble the work, influence the agents, and evaluate responses.

Depending on your brand’s sector or stage of development, these are some of the agentic roles organizations might need.

Advisor: Helps with high level information to navigate options and choices. Beauty brands for instance do this routinely if a favorite product has been reformulated. Brands getting into agentic commerce need to consider being more aggressive updating information whenever there’s a change.

Assistant: Anticipates various use cases and offers personalized options across categories. Think of how retailers organize party planning décor, food, and beverage by occasion. Brands need to think about how to structure information about their entire products in a similar fashion.

Operator: Creates a one stop shop with enough information to empower agents to do the heavy-lifting around shopping and buying. Think of how airlines compile an entire travel itinerary with locations, dates, prices and special activities all the way through to booking the trip.

It’s important to remember that the agentic advisor is not just content management; the agentic assistant is not just bookmarking the shopping cart; and the agentic operator isn’t just showing promos for related products and services.

These agents should act more like a customer’s BFF. They listen to preferences and eventually complete the purchase. And these roles shape the brand story.

Marketers today emphasize AI for efficiency in creative, performance, and process, but they must now deepen that scope to include agentic commerce.

Gartner forecasts 20% of commerce will be agentic by 2030, which coincides with predicted 20% adoption of programmable money, or digital funds guided by a framework of rules and safeguards.

This means the terms of purchase can be automated too. That combination of commercial change is seismic, as agents decide whether your product or service is right for a consumer.

Who better to navigate customer insight than CMOs, creative leaders with expertise in translating brand value? As storytellers, these skills can creatively power the team’s new relationship with agents designed to learn about your brand. As the flurry of AI headlines continues, a clear path exists for marketers and media leaders to bring brand storytelling and agentic commerce closer together.

Karin Timpone is CEO and founder of ClearPrompt, a business strategy advisory with proprietary AI tools that meld human wisdom and technology acceleration. Previously CMO for Marriott and MLB, Karin held senior executive roles at Disney, Yahoo!, Universal Studios, and Seagram.

Much like SEO, brands must know how to optimize for AI platforms

#7329. ChatGPT Is Going From Healthcare Advice to Prescriptions Platform

This week Photon, a digital prescription network and pharmacy marketplace, announced it is adding a prescribing plugin for ChatGPT. Photon’s plugin launched on November 16. It can be used by U.S. clinicians using ChatGPT for Clinicians or ChatGPT for Healthcare, OpenAI’s LLM products for clinicians and healthcare organizations.

The plugin allows clinicians to draft, review, and send prescriptions from ChatGPT.

Clinicians using the plugin can search the name of a medication they would like to prescribe. The plugin will then provide dosing recommendations and draft prescriptions based on the patient’s information and context provided by ChatGPT.

Details like patient allergies and medication history are taken into consideration and new details can be added as the prescription is being created. Each prescription requires verification from the clinician before being sent to a patient.

Once the prescription is sent, patients receive a link which allows them to compare pharmacies and decide where to fill the prescription.

“The goal is to take administrative work off the clinician’s plate so they can focus on care.” said Otto Sipe, CEO and co-founder of Photon. “That’s what human agency means here: the clinician controls the prescription, and the patient chooses where to fill it.”

According to Photon’s co-founder, platforms like Photon are ensuring its services can be accessed in different ways.

“Making our infrastructure accessible to clinicians and the agents they use gives us another way to reach them.” said Sipe. “As more treatment workflows happen in these interfaces, we want Photon’s prescribing tools and marketplace to be available there.”
